\ell\leftarrow\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{\ell}}{12\cdot\Theta(\gamma)}\right\rfloor
.
With some extra calculations, we can show that
\ell+1=\Omega(\hat{\ell}+1)=\Omega(\ell^{\star}+1)
and

\frac{\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-{\ell}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})}{\Theta(\gamma)}\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 4\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+{\ell}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

The running time of this
for
loop is
\tilde{O}\left(n\sum_{j=0}^{i^{\star}}s_{j}\right)=\tilde{O}(ns_{i^{\star}})
, where
i^{\star}
is the index s.t.
s_{i^{\star}-1}=\hat{\ell}
. Thus, we have
s_{i^{\star}}=O(\hat{\ell})=O(\ell+1)
, and hence we can implement Step 1 in
\tilde{O}(n\cdot(\ell+1))
time.

Implementing Step 2.

Instead of finding the optimum set of
(k-\ell)
centers within
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
, we approximate it using
Lemma

6.1
:
We compute a set of
(k-\ell)
centers
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq O(1)\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-\ell}^{\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
With the same arguments as before, we get
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq O(1)\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+\ell}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
Note that the running time for this step is also
\tilde{O}(n\cdot(\ell+1))
.

Implementing Step 3.

Trivially, we can implement each of these updates in constant time.

Implementing Step 4.

We first need to add
O(\ell+1)
centers to
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
, while minimizing the cost of the solution w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
We compute an approximation of
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, by setting
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
,
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
and
s=\Theta(\gamma\cdot(\ell+1))
in
Lemma

6.2
below (see
Equation

9
). This also takes
\tilde{O}(n\cdot(\ell+1))
time. We defer the proof sketch of
Lemma

6.2
to
Section

6.2.2
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Suppose that we have access to the auxiliary data structure described above (see
Section

6.1
).
Then, given any integer
s\geq 1
, in
\tilde{O}(ns)
time we can find a superset
\mathcal{U}^{\star}\supseteq\mathcal{U}
of
(k+s)
centers such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\star},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq O(1)\cdot\min\limits_{\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathbf{P}:|\mathcal{F}|\leq s}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}\right)
.

At this stage, we compute
\mathcal{V}^{\star}:=\mathcal{U}^{\star}+\left(\mathcal{P}^{(\ell+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)
as in
Equation

10
, in only
\tilde{O}(\ell+1)
time.
Next, we compute an approximation of
\mathcal{W}^{\star}
(see
Equation

10
) using
Lemma

6.1
, which again takes
\tilde{O}(n\cdot(\ell+1))
time. It follows that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\star},\mathcal{P}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\leq O(1)\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(\ell+1)})
.
Finally, we explain below how we implement the call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
(see
Equation

11
).

6.2.1
Implementing the calls to
\textsc{Robustify}(\cdot)
subroutine

Recall
Algorithm

1
and
Algorithm

2
from
Section

3.2
. In the static setting, there are known
O(1)
-approximation algorithms for
1
-median with
\tilde{O}(n)
runtime
[
MP02
]
. Using any such
1
-median algorithm, it is relatively straightforward to (approximately) implement a call to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
in
\tilde{O}(n)
time (see
Algorithm

1
in
Algorithm

1
). Using the auxiliary data structure (see
Section

6.1
), it is easy to implement each invocation of
Algorithm

1
in
Algorithm

1
in
\tilde{O}(1)
time, whereas
Algorithm

1
in
Algorithm

1
trivially takes
\tilde{O}(1)
time. By
Lemma

5.8
, our dynamic algorithm makes
\tilde{O}(1)
many amortized calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
, per update. Thus, it follows that we spend
\tilde{O}(n)
amortized time per update on implementing
Algorithms

1
,
1
and
1
in
Algorithm

1
.

There remains a significant challenge: We might have to iterate over
\left|\mathcal{W}\right|=k
centers in
Algorithm

1
in
Algorithm

1
, before we find a center
w\in\mathcal{W}
that violates
Equation

4
. Let us refer to this operation as “testing a center
w
”; this occurs when we check whether
w
violates
Equation

4
. In other words, we need to perform
O(k)
many such tests in
Algorithm

1
, before we execute a
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
call in
Algorithm

1
in
Algorithm

1
. Note that because of
Lemma

5.8
, we perform
\tilde{O}(k)
many tests, on average, per update. Thus, if we could hypothetically perform each test in
\tilde{O}(1)
time, then we would incur an additive overhead of
\tilde{O}(k)
in our amortized update time, and everything would be fine.

The issue, however, is that testing a center can be prohibitively expensive. This is because
Equation

4
consists of two conditions.
The second condition (which finds the value of
t
) requires us to know the value of
d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)
, and this can indeed be implemented in
\tilde{O}(1)
time using our auxiliary data structure (see
Section

6.1
).
The first condition asks us to check whether
w
is
t
-robust, and there does not seem to be
any
efficient way in which we can implement this check (see
Definition

3.2
).
To address this significant challenge, we modify the execution of a call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
at the end of an epoch, as described below.

Modified version of the call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
.

At the end of an epoch, the call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
is supposed to return the set
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{final}}}
(see
Equation

11
). We replace this call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
by the procedure in
Algorithm

4
below.

To appreciate what
Algorithm

4
does, recall the recourse analysis in
Section

5.3
; in particular, the distinction between
contaminated
vs
clean
centers in
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
, and the three types of calls to the
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
subroutine. Note that in
Algorithms

4
,
4
and
4
in
Algorithm

4
, the sets
\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{3}
respectively correspond to those centers
w
that might
potentially
be the sources of Type I, Type II and Type III calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(w,\cdot)
. We refer to the centers in
\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{3}
respectively as Type I, Type II and Type III centers.

The key difference between
Algorithm

4
and the previous version of
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
is this: In
Algorithm

4
, we proactively make calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(w\,,\cdot)
without even checking the first condition in
Equation

4
, which was the main bottleneck in achieving efficient update time. To be more specific, we proactively call
\textsc{Make-Robust}(w,\cdot)
for every Type I and Type II center
w
(see
Algorithm

4
).
In contrast, for a Type III center
w
, we call
\textsc{Make-Robust}(w,\cdot)
whenever we observe that
t>t[w]
, where
t
is the smallest integer satisfying
10^{t}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/200
(see
Algorithms

4
and
4
). Note that if
t\leq t[w]
in
Algorithm

4
, then by
Claim

5.9
the center
w
does
not
violate
Equation

4
.

Algorithm 4

Modified version of the call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
at the end of an epoch.

1

\mathcal{W}_{1}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}^{\star}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}

2

\mathcal{W}_{2}\leftarrow\{w\in\mathcal{W}^{\star}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}:w\text{ is contaminated}\}

3

\mathcal{W}_{3}\leftarrow\{w\in\mathcal{W}^{\star}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}:w\text{ is clean}\}

//
The set
\mathcal{W}^{\star}
is partitioned into the subsets
\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2},\mathcal{W}_{3}

4

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}^{\star}

5

for

each center
w\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\cup\mathcal{W}_{2}

do

6

t\leftarrow
Smallest integer satisfying
10^{t}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/100

7

w_{0}\leftarrow

Make-Robust
(w,t)

8

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}-w+w_{0}

9

Save
t[w_{0}]\leftarrow t
and
p_{t[w_{0}]}(w_{0})\leftarrow w
(see
Section

5.3
) together with
w_{0}

10

while

true

do

11

for

each center
w\in\mathcal{W}_{3}

do

12

t\leftarrow
Smallest integer satisfying
10^{t}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/200

13

if

t>t[w]

then

Smallest integer satisfying

w_{0}\leftarrow

(w,t^{\prime})

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}-w+w_{0}

17

Save

and

(see

) together with
w_{0}

4
.

return
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 6.3

(Informal)
.

Lemma

5.8
continues to hold even after the call to
\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{W}^{\star})
at the end of every epoch is replaced by the procedure in
Algorithm

4
.

Proof.

(Sketch)
The lemma holds because the procedure in
Algorithm

4
is perfectly aligned with the recourse analysis in
Section

5.3
. In other words, the recourse analysis accounts for the scenario where we make proactive calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
, precisely in the manner specified by
Algorithm

4
. For example, the recourse analysis bounds the number of Type I and Type II calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
, by pretending that every Type I or Type II center makes such a call, regardless of whether or not it violates
Equation

4
.
∎

Bounding the Update Time.
First, note that we can implement each invocation of
Algorithms

4
,
4
,
4
and
4
in
Algorithm

4
in
\tilde{O}(k\cdot(\ell+1))
time, which gets amortized over the length of the epoch, as an epoch lasts for
(\ell+1)
updates. Specifically, to compute the sets
\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{3}
, we iterate over all
w\in\mathcal{W}^{\star}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
and
q\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(\ell+1)}
, and check whether the update involving
q
contaminates
w
, using the value
t[w]
and the point
p_{t[w]}(w)
.
Moreover,
Algorithms

4
and
4
trivially take
O(k)
time.

Using the auxiliary data structure (see
Section

6.1
), we can implement each invocation of
Algorithms

4
,
4
and
4
in
\tilde{O}(1)
time. Next, say that an iteration of the
for
loop in
Algorithm

4
is
uninterrupted
if we find that
t\leq t[w]
in
Algorithm

4

(and accordingly do not execute any line within the
if
block) and
interrupted
otherwise.
Each uninterrupted iteration of the
for
loop takes
\tilde{O}(1)
time. Furthermore, there can be at most

|\mathcal{W}_{3}|=O(k)
many
consecutive
uninterrupted iterations of the
for
loop in
Algorithm

4
: Any such chain of uninterrupted iterations is broken (i) either by an interrupted iteration, which involves a call to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(w,t^{\prime})
in
Algorithm

4
, (ii) or by the termination of the procedure in
Algorithm

4
(this can happen only once in an epoch).

From the preceding discussion, it follows that total time spent on the remaining lines in
Algorithm

4
is dominated by the time spent on the calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
. Recall that at the start of
Section

6.2.1
, we have already explained that we spend
\tilde{O}(n)
time to implement each call to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
. Finally,
Lemmas

6.3
and
5.8
imply that we make
\tilde{O}(1)
amortized calls to
\textsc{Make-Robust}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
, per update. This gives us an overall amortized update time of
\tilde{O}(n)
.

6.2.2
Algorithm for
Lemma

6.2

Since we have a set of fixed
k
centers
\mathcal{U}
that must be contained in
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
,
if we can treat these fixed centers as a single center instead of a set of
k
centers, we might be able to reduce the problem to a
(s+1)
-median problem.
So, we contract all of the points
\mathcal{U}
to a single point
u^{\star}
and define a new space
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{\star}
with a new metric
d^{\prime}
as follows.

\begin{cases}d^{\prime}(x,u^{\star}):=d(x,\mathcal{U})&\forall x\in\mathcal{P}\\
d^{\prime}(x,y):=\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{\star})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{\star}),d(x,y)\}&\forall x,y\in\mathcal{P}\end{cases}

(25)

This function
d^{\prime}
defines a metric on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
A simple way to verify this is that
d^{\prime}
is the metric derived from the shortest path in a complete graph where weight of edges between any two nodes in
\mathcal{U}
is zero and the weight of the other edges is the
d
distance of their endpoints (you can find a complete proof in
Claim

11.2
).
We also define weights for each
x\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
We define the weight of all
x\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}-u^{\star}
to be
1
and the weight of
u^{\star}
to be very large denoted by
\infty
to enforce any constant approximate solution for
(s+1)
-median in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to open center
u^{\star}
.

In order to have access to metric
d^{\prime}
, we can simply construct an oracle
D^{\prime}
to compute it.
This is because we have access to sorted distances of any
p\in\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{U}
through
\mathcal{T}_{p}
in our auxiliary data structure.
Combining with the definition of
d^{\prime}
, it is easy to see that we can compute
d^{\prime}(x,y)
in
O(1)
time for each
x,y\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

Next, we run the algorithm of
[
MP02
]
for
(s+1)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
w.r.t. metric
d^{\prime}
to find a
\mathcal{F}
of size at most
(s+1)
which is a constant approximation for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{s+1}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
in a total of
\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|\cdot(s+1))=\tilde{O}(n\cdot(\ell+1))
time.
Finally, we let
\mathcal{U}^{\star}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{F}-u^{\star})
.
Note that
\mathcal{F}-u^{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and its size is at most
s
since
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
.
This set
\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{F}-u^{\star})
is going to be a good solution w.r.t. metric
d
as well as
d^{\prime}
(see
Lemma

11.3
).

Algorithm 5

Computing an approximation of the optimum
\mathcal{U}^{\star}\supseteq\mathcal{U}
of size
(k+s)
.

1

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{\star}
.

2

w(u^{\star})\leftarrow\infty
,
w(x)\leftarrow 1\ \forall x\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}-u^{\star}
.

3

Consider
D^{\prime}
as an oracle to the metric
d^{\prime}
defined in
Equation

25
.

4

Compute any
O(1)
approximate solution
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for
(s+1)
-median problem on weighted metric space
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in
\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|\cdot(s+1))
time using
[
MP02
]
with access to distance oracle
D^{\prime}
.

5

return

\leftarrow\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{F}-u^{\star})
.

6.3
Improving the Update Time to
\tilde{O}(k)

Extension to Weighted Case.

First, we argue that our algorithm can be extended to the weighted case defined as follows.
We have a metric space
\mathcal{P}
with positive weights
w(p)
for each
p\in\mathcal{P}
and distance function
d
. Denote this weighted space by
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
.
The cost of a collection
\mathcal{U}
of
k
centers is defined as
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)\cdot d(p,\mathcal{U})
and subsequently
\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(\mathcal{U},S\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},S\right)/(\sum_{p\in S}w(p))
for all
S\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.
We can extend our algorithm and all of the arguments for weighted case.

Sparsification.

Note that parameter
n
in our algorithm is the maximum size of the space at any time during the total sequence of updates (it is
not
the size of the underlying ground metric space
\mathbf{P}
).
As a result, if we make sure that the size of the space
\mathcal{P}
is at most
\tilde{O}(k)
at any point in time, then the amortized running time of the algorithm would be
\tilde{O}(k)
as desired.

We use the result of
[
BCLP23
]
to sparsify the input.
A simple generalization of this result is presented in Section 10 of
[
BCG+24
]
which extends this sparsifier to weighted metric spaces.
The authors provided an algorithm to sparsify the space to
\tilde{O}(k)
weighted points.
More precisely, given a dynamic metric space
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
and parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an algorithm that maintains a dynamic metric space
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
in
\tilde{O}(k)
amortized update time such that the following holds.

•

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and the size of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
at any time is
\tilde{O}(k)
.

•

A sequence of
T
updates in
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
, leads to a sequence of
O(T)
updates in
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
.
13
13

13

This guarantee follows from a slightly more refined analysis of the recourse of this sparsifier which is presented in
[
BCLP24
]
(see Lemma 3.4 of
[
BCLP24
]
).

•

Every
\alpha
approximate solution to the
k
-median problem in the metric space
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
is also a
O(\alpha)
approximate solution to the
k
-median problem in the metric space

(\mathcal{P},w,d)
with probability at least
1-\tilde{O}(1/n^{c})
.

Now, suppose that we are given a sequence of updates
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{T}
in a dynamic metric space
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
.
Instead of feeding the metric space
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
directly to our algorithm, we can
perform this dynamic sparsification to obtain a sequence of updates
\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{T^{\prime}}
for a metric space
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
, where
T^{\prime}=O(T)
, and feed the metric space
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
to our dynamic algorithm instead.
Since our algorithm maintains a
O(1)
approximate solution
\mathcal{U}
to the
k
-median problem in
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},w^{\prime},d)
, then
\mathcal{U}
is also a
O(1)
approximate solution for the
k
-median problem in
(\mathcal{P},w,d)
with high probability.

Since the length of the stream is multiplied by
O(1)
, we would have a multiplicative overhead of
O(1)
in the amortized update time and recourse (amortized w.r.t. the original input stream).

7
Missing Proofs From Section
4

7.1
Proof of Lemma
4.1
: Double-Sided Stability Lemma

Consider the LP relaxation for improper
k
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}
for each
k
as follows.

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{c\in\mathbf{P}}d(c,p)\cdot x_{cp}

s.t.

\displaystyle\quad x_{cp}\leq y_{c}

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathbf{P},p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathbf{P}}x_{cp}\geq 1

\displaystyle\forall p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathbf{P}}y_{c}\leq k

\displaystyle x_{cp},y_{c}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathbf{P},p\in\mathcal{P}

Denote the cost of the optimal fractional solution for this LP by
\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}
. Since space
\mathcal{P}
is fixed here, we denote
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
by
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
.
It is known that the integrality gap of this relaxation is at most
3

[
CS11
]
. So, for every
k
we have

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\cdot\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}.

(26)

Claim 7.1
.

For every
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
and
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 1
such that
\alpha+\beta=1
, we have

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}\leq\alpha\cdot\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\cdot\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}.

Proof.

Assume optimal fractional solutions
(x^{*}_{1},y^{*}_{1})
and
(x^{*}_{2},y^{*}_{2})
to be an optimal solution for above LP for fractional
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
-median problems respectively. It is easy to verify that their convex combination
(\alpha x^{*}_{1}+\beta x^{*}_{2},\alpha y^{*}_{1}+\beta y^{*}_{2})
is a feasible solution for LP relaxation of
(\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2})
-median problem whose cost is
\alpha\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}
which concludes the claim.
∎

Now, plug
k_{1}=k-r
,
k_{2}=k+r/(12\eta)
,
\alpha=1/(12\eta)
and
\beta=1-\alpha
in the claim. We have

\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}=\frac{1}{12\eta}(k-r)+\left(1-\frac{1}{12\eta}\right)\left(k+\frac{r}{12\eta}\right)=k-\frac{r}{(12\eta)^{2}}\leq k.

As a result,

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}\leq\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}\leq\alpha\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}
.
Together with
Equation

26
, we have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\alpha\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{1}}+3\beta\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}
.
We also have the assumption that
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{1}}=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-r}\leq\eta\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
, which implies

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\alpha\eta\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}+3\beta\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}
.
Finally,

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq\left(\frac{3\beta}{1-3\alpha\eta}\right)\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}\leq 4\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}\leq 4\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+\lfloor r/(12\eta)\rfloor}.

The second inequality holds since

\frac{3\beta}{1-3\alpha\eta}\leq\frac{3}{1-3\alpha\eta}=\frac{3}{1-1/4}=4.

7.2
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Consider the standard LP relaxation for the weighted
k
-median problem as follows.

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}d(u,p)\cdot x_{up}

s.t.

\displaystyle\quad x_{up}\leq y_{u}

\displaystyle\forall u\in\mathcal{U},p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}x_{up}\geq 1

\displaystyle\forall p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}y_{u}\leq k-(m-r)/4

\displaystyle x_{up},y_{u}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall u\in\mathcal{U},p\in\mathcal{P}

We consider the set of potential centers to open is
\mathcal{U}
, and we want to open at most
k-(m-r)/4
many centers.
Since the integrality gap of this LP is known to be at most
3

[
CS11
]
, it suffices to show the existence of a fractional solution whose cost is at most
2\gamma\cdot\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right)

and this solution opens at most

k-(m-r)/4
centers.
Now, we explain how to construct a fractional solution for this LP.

Fractional Opening of Centers.

Consider the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
function.
Assume
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{I}+\mathcal{U}_{F}
is a partition of
\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}_{I}
contains those centers
u\in\mathcal{U}
satisfying at least one of the following conditions:

•

u
forms a well-separated pair with one center in
\mathcal{V}
.

•

|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq 2
.

For every
u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}
, set
y_{u}=1
and for every
u\in\mathcal{U}_{F}
set
y_{u}=1/2
.
Each center
u\in\mathcal{U}
that forms a well-separated pair with a center
v\in\mathcal{V}
has
|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq 1
since
u
must be the closest center to
v
in
\mathcal{U}
.
Since the number of well-separated pairs is
k-m
, we have

k+r=|\mathcal{V}|=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}}|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq 1\cdot(k-m)+2\cdot\left(|\mathcal{U}_{I}|-(k-m)\right).

Hence,

|\mathcal{U}_{I}|\leq\frac{k+r+(k-m)}{2}=k-\frac{m-r}{2}
.
Finally, we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}y_{u}=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}}y_{u}+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{F}}y_{u}\leq 1\cdot\left(k-\frac{m-r}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{m-r}{2}=k-\frac{m-r}{4}.

Fractional Assignment of Points.

For every
p\in\mathcal{P}
, assume
v_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{V}}(p)
is the closest center to
p
in
\mathcal{V}
and
u_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})
is the closest center in
\mathcal{U}
to
v_{p}
.
We have three cases:

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1
, then set
x_{u_{p}p}=1
. The cost of this assignment would be
d(u_{p},p)
.

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
and there is a center
u^{\prime}_{p}\in\mathcal{U}-u_{p}
such that
d(u_{p},u^{\prime}_{p})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
, then set
x_{u_{p}p}=x_{u_{p}^{\prime}p}=1/2
. Note that
u_{p}^{\prime}\neq u_{p}
which means this solution is feasible to the LP.
The cost of this assignment would be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u^{\prime}_{p},u_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(p,u_{p})+\frac{\gamma}{2}\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p}).

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
and the previous case does not hold, then since
(u_{p},v_{p})
is not a well-separated pair, there is a center
v^{\prime}_{p}\in\mathcal{V}-v_{p}
such that
d(v_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
. Let
u_{p}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v^{\prime}_{p})
and set
x_{u_{p}p}=x_{u^{\prime}_{p}p}=1/2
. First, we show that
u^{\prime}_{p}\neq u_{p}
. Since
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
, we have
u_{p}\in\mathcal{U}_{F}
which concludes
|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{p})|\leq 1
.
We also know that
\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})=u_{p}
.
So,
v_{p}
is the only center in
\mathcal{V}
mapped to
u_{p}
which implies

\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v^{\prime}_{p})\neq u_{p}
or
u_{p}^{\prime}\neq u_{p}
(note that
v_{p}^{\prime}\neq v_{p}
). So, point
p
is assigned one unit to centers.
The cost of this assignment would be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u^{\prime}_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})+d(v^{\prime}_{p},u^{\prime}_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})+d(v^{\prime}_{p},u_{p})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v_{p})+d(v_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p}).

The second inequality is because of the choice of
u^{\prime}_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p}^{\prime})
and the last inequality is because
d(v_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
.

Bounding the Cost.

Assume
u_{p}^{*}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(p)
for each
p\in\mathcal{P}
.
We have

d(u_{p},v_{p})\leq d(u_{p}^{*},v_{p})\leq d(u_{p}^{*},p)+d(p,v_{p}).

The first inequality is by the choice of
u_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})
.
As a result,

\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}d(u_{p},v_{p})\leq\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\left(d(u^{*}_{p},p)+d(p,v_{p})\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right).

(27)

In each of the cases of fractional assignments of points to centers, the cost of assigning a point
p\in C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})
is at most

d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
.
As a result, the total cost of this assignment is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in P}\left(d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\left(d(p,v_{p})+(\gamma+2)\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)+(\gamma+2)\cdot\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\gamma\cdot\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right).

The second inequality follows by
Equation

27
.

Part II
Proofs That Follow From Prior Work

8
Missing Proofs From Section
3

8.1
Proof of
Lemma

3.3

The lemma is obvious if
\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)\geq 10^{j}/5
, because
p_{j}=p_{j-1}
which means
d(p_{j-1},p_{j})=0
.
Now, assume
\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)<10^{j}/5
. By the definition of a
t
-robust sequence, we know

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle d(p_{j},p_{j-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|B_{j}|}\cdot\sum_{p\in B_{j}}d(p_{j},p_{j-1})\leq\frac{1}{|B_{j}|}\cdot\sum_{p\in B_{j}}(d(p_{j},p)+d(p_{j-1},p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)+\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)\leq\frac{2}{5}\cdot 10^{j}.

For every
p\in B_{j-1}
we have

d(p,p_{j})\leq d(p,p_{j-1})+d(p_{j-1},p_{j})\leq 10^{j-1}+\frac{2}{5}\cdot 10^{j}\leq 10^{j},

which implies
B_{j-1}\subseteq B_{j}
.
Finally,

d(p_{0},p_{j})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}d(p_{i-1},p_{i})\leq\frac{2}{5}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{j}10^{i}=\frac{2}{5}\cdot\frac{10^{j+1}-2}{9}\leq 10^{j}/2.

8.2
Proof of
Lemma

3.4

Since
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
is
t
-robust, for every
1\leq j\leq t
, we know

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)\geq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right).

(28)

Assume
q\in\mathcal{P}\setminus B_{j}
. Then, by
Lemma

3.3
,

d(q,p_{j})\geq 10^{j}\geq 2\cdot d(p_{0},p_{j})
.
Hence,

\frac{3}{2}\cdot d(q,p_{j})=d(q,p_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot d(q,p_{j})\geq d(q,p_{j})+d(p_{0},p_{j})\geq d(q,p_{0}),

which means

d(q,p_{j})\geq\frac{2}{3}\cdot d(q,p_{0}).

(29)

Finally, Since
B_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq B_{i}\subseteq S
(by
Lemma

3.3
) we can apply
Equation

28
and
Equation

29
repeatedly to get

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},S\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},B_{i}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},S\setminus B_{i}\right)\geq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i-1}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i}\setminus B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-2},B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},B_{i}\setminus B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-2},B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},B_{1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{1}\right)\geq\frac{2}{3}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\right).

8.3
Proof of
Lemma

3.6

Assume a
Make-Robust
call to a center
w\in\mathcal{W}
is made and it is replaced by
w_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{1}
be the set of centers just before this call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w
.
At this point of time,
t[w_{0}]
is the smallest integer satisfying
10^{t[w_{0}]}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}_{1}-w)/100
(note that
t[w_{0}]\geq 0
, otherwise the algorithm should not have called
Make-Robust
duo to line 10 in the
Robustify
).
For the sake of contradiction, assume that another call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w_{0}
.
We assume that this pair
w
,
w_{0}
is the first pair for which this occurs.
Let
w^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{W}_{1}-w}(w)
which means
d(w,\mathcal{W}_{1}-w)=d(w,w^{\prime})
.
Then,

10^{t[w_{0}]}\geq d(w,w^{\prime})/100
and
10^{t[w_{0}]}<d(w,w^{\prime})/10
by definition of
t[w_{0}]
. So, by
Lemma

10.3
, we have

d(w_{0},w)\leq 10^{t[w_{0}]}/2\leq d(w,w^{\prime})/20.

(30)

Assume
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the set of centers just after the call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w_{0}
.
Since we assumed
w,w_{0}
is the first pair that another call to
Make-Robust
on
w_{0}
is made, we have one of the two following cases.

•

w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{2}
.
In this case, we have

d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})\leq d(w_{0},w^{\prime})\leq d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})\leq\frac{21}{20}\cdot d(w,w^{\prime})\leq 2\cdot d(w,w^{\prime}).

The last inequality holds by
Equation

30
.
This is in contradiction with the assumption that a call to
Make-Robust
on
w_{0}
is made, because
w_{0}
is
t[w_{0}]
-robust where

10^{t[w_{0}]}\geq d(w,w^{\prime})/100\geq d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})/200
. So,
w_{0}
must not be selected to be added to
\mathcal{S}
in
Robustify
.

•

There is
w^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{2}
that replaced
w^{\prime}
by a single call to
Make-Robust
.
When
Make-Robust
is called on
w^{\prime}
, the algorithm picks integer
t[w_{0}^{\prime}]
that satisfies

10^{t[w_{0}^{\prime}]}\leq d(w^{\prime},w_{0})/10
(otherwise, it should have picked
t[w_{0}^{\prime}]-1
instead of
t[w_{0}^{\prime}]
).
Hence,

d(w_{0}^{\prime},w^{\prime})\leq 10^{t[w_{0}^{\prime}]}/2\leq d(w^{\prime},w_{0})/20\leq\left(d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})\right)/20\leq d(w,w^{\prime})/10.

(31)

The first inequality holds by
Lemma

10.3
and the last inequality is
Equation

30
.
As a result,

\displaystyle d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(w_{0},w_{0}^{\prime})\leq d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})+d(w^{\prime},w_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(w,w^{\prime})/20+d(w,w^{\prime})+d(w,w^{\prime})/10

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{23}{20}\cdot d(w,w^{\prime})\leq 2\cdot d(w,w^{\prime}).

The last inequality holds by
Equation

30
and
Equation

31
.
This leads to the same contradiction.

8.4
Proof of
Lemma

3.7

By
Lemma

3.6
,
Robustify
calls
Make-Robust
on each center at most once.
Let
r
be the number of centers in
\mathcal{W}
for which a call to
Make-Robust
has happened.
Assume

\mathcal{W}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}
is an ordering by the time that
Make-Robust
is called on the centers
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{r}
and the last elements are ordered arbitrarily.
Assume also that
\mathcal{U}=\{w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{k}\}
where
w_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained by the call
Make-Robust
on
w_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
and
w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}
for each
r+1\leq i\leq k
.

For every
1\leq j\leq r
, integer
t[w_{j}^{\prime}]
is the smallest integer satisfying

10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}\geq d(w_{j},\{w_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,w_{j-1}^{\prime},w_{j+1},\ldots,w_{k}\})/100.

Hence,

10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}\leq d(w_{j},w_{i}^{\prime})/10\quad\forall i<j

(32)

and

10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}\leq d(w_{j},w_{i})/10\quad\forall i>j.

(33)

Now, for every
1\leq i<j\leq r
, we conclude

10\cdot 10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}\leq d(w_{i}^{\prime},w_{j})\leq d(w_{i},w_{j})+d(w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime})\leq d(w_{i},w_{j})+10^{t[w_{i}^{\prime}]}/2\leq\frac{11}{10}\cdot d(w_{i},w_{j}).

The first inequality holds by
Equation

32
, the third one holds by
Lemma

3.3
(note that
w_{i}^{\prime}=

Make-Robust
(w_{i})
) and the last one holds by
Equation

33
(by exchanging
i
and
j
in this equation since
i<j
).
This concludes for each
i<j
, we have
d(w_{i},w_{j})>2\cdot 10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}
.
The same inequality holds for each
i>j
by
Equation

33
.
As a result,

d(w_{i},w_{j})>2\cdot 10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}
for all
i\neq j
.
This concludes

\text{Ball}_{10^{t[w_{j}^{\prime}]}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{j})\subseteq C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}),

for every
1\leq j\leq r
.
Then, we can apply
Lemma

3.4
, we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j}^{\prime},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq r.

(34)

Finally, since
C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})
for
1\leq j\leq k
form a partition of
\mathcal{P}
, we conclude

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j}^{\prime},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j}^{\prime},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)+\sum_{j=r+1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j}^{\prime},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{r}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)+\sum_{j=r+1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)=\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}\right).

The second inequality holds by
Equation

34
.

8.5
Proof of
Lemma

3.9

Since, the point-set
\mathcal{P}
is fixed, denote
C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})
by
C_{v}
for simplicity.
If
d(u,v)=0
, the statement is trivial.
Now assume
d(u,v)\geq 1
.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is robust, then
u
is
t
-robust where
t
is the smallest integer such that

10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{U}-u)/200\geq(\gamma/200)\cdot d(u,v)=20\cdot d(u,v)
.
The second inequality is because
(u,v)
is well-separated pair and the equality is because
\gamma=4000
.
Assume
t^{*}
is the integer satisfying

20\cdot d(u,v)\leq 10^{t^{*}}<10\cdot 20\cdot d(u,v).

(35)

Note that
t^{*}\geq 1
. Since
t^{*}\leq t
and
u
is
t
-robust, then it is also
t^{*}
-robust which means there is a
t^{*}
-robust sequence

(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t^{*}})
such that
p_{0}=u
.
For Simplicity, assume
B_{i}=\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p_{i})
for
0\leq i\leq t^{*}
.

Claim 8.1
.

For each
0\leq i\leq t^{*}
, we have
B_{i}\subseteq C_{v}
.

Proof.

Assume
q\in B_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle d(q,v)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(q,p_{i})+d(p_{i},u)+d(u,v)=d(q,p_{i})+d(p_{i},p_{0})+d(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{i}+10^{i}/2+10^{t^{*}}/20\leq 2\cdot 10^{t^{*}}.

For the second inequality, we used
q\in B_{i}
,
Lemma

3.3
and
Equation

35
.
Now, for every
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}-v
we have

d(v,v^{\prime})\geq d(v,\mathcal{V}-v)\geq 4\cdot 10^{3}\cdot d(u,v)>10^{t^{*}+1}\geq 2\cdot d(q,v)
.
The second inequality is by
(u,v)
being well-separated (and
\gamma=4000
), the third one is by
Equation

35
.
This concludes
d(q,v^{\prime})\geq d(v,v^{\prime})-d(q,v)>d(q,v)
which means
v
is the closest center to
q
in
\mathcal{V}
. So,
q\in B_{i}
and finally
B_{i}\subseteq C_{v}
.
∎

Now, according to the definition of
t
-robust sequence we have two cases.

Case 1:

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq 10^{t^{*}}/5
. In this case, we have
p_{t^{*}-1}=p_{t^{*}}
and

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq d(v,p_{0})+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})=d(v,u)+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{t^{*}}/20+10^{t^{*}-1}/2=10^{t^{*}}/10.

The second inequality holds by
Lemma

3.3
and
Equation

35
.
So,

d(v,p_{t^{*}})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10.

(36)

For every
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
, we have

d(q,v)\geq d(p_{t^{*}},q)-d(v,p_{t^{*}})\geq 10^{t^{*}}-d(v,p_{t^{*}})\geq d(v,p_{t^{*}})
.
The second inequality follows by
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
and the last inequality follows by

Equation

36
.
So,

d(q,v)\geq(d(q,v)+d(v,p_{t^{*}}))/2\geq d(q,p_{t^{*}})/2
,
which implies

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}.

(37)

We also have

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq 10^{t^{*}}/5\geq 2\cdot d(v,p_{t^{*}}),

(38)

by the assumption of this case and
Equation

36
.
Now, we conclude

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|B_{t^{*}}|\cdot\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|B_{t^{*}}|\cdot(\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)-d(v,p_{t^{*}}))+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|B_{t^{*}}|\cdot\frac{\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}=\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}\right)}{2}.

The first inequality is triangle inequality, the second inequality holds by
Equation

38
and the last inequality holds by
Equation

37
.
Finally, since
B_{t^{*}}\subseteq C_{v}
(by
Claim

8.1
), we can apply
Lemma

3.4
to get

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)=3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right).

Case 2:

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)<10^{t^{*}}/5
. In this case, for every
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
,

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\geq d(q,p_{t^{*}})-d(p_{t^{*}},p_{t^{*}-1})\geq 10^{t^{*}}-10^{t^{*}}/2=10^{t^{*}}/2.

The second inequality holds by
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
and
Lemma

3.3
.
So,

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\geq 10^{t^{*}}/2.

(39)

We also have

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq d(v,u)+d(u,p_{t^{*}-1})=d(v,u)+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/20+10^{t^{*}-1}/2=10^{t^{*}}/10.

The second inequality holds by
Equation

35
and
Lemma

3.3
.
So,

d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10.

(40)

Combining
Equation

39
and
Equation

40
, we have

d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10=\frac{1}{5}\cdot 10^{t^{*}}/2\leq\frac{1}{5}\cdot d(q,p_{t^{*}-1}),

which concludes

\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,v)}\leq\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})-d(p_{t^{*}-1},v)}\leq\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})-\frac{1}{5}\cdot d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}=5/4\leq 2.

So, we have

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 2\cdot d(q,v)
.
Using this inequality for all
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
, we conclude

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)\leq 2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right).

(41)

We also have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},B_{t^{*}}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}^{B_{t^{*}}+p_{t^{*}}}_{1}(B_{t^{*}})\leq 2\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B_{t^{*}})\leq 2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right).

(42)

The equality holds by the definition of
p_{t^{*}-1}
in a
t^{*}
-robust sequence, the first inequality holds by
Lemma

3.10
and
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(B_{t^{*}}+p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)=0
(we need this argument since we do not know if
v
is inside the current space
\mathcal{P}
).
Hence,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)=2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right).

The equality holds since
B_{t^{*}}\subseteq C_{v}
(by
Claim

8.1
) and
the first inequality holds by
Equation

41
and
Equation

42
.
Finally, applying
Lemma

3.4
implies

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)=3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right).

In both cases, we showed that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)\leq 3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)
which completes the proof.

8.6
Proof of
Lemma

3.10

Let
\mathcal{V}^{\star}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
be of size
k
such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\star},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
.
Assume
\mathcal{V}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{V}^{\star})
.
Obviously
\mathcal{V}
is of size at most
k
and
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}^{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{P})\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)
.
For each
p\in\mathcal{P}
, let
v_{p}^{\star}
and
u_{p}
be the projection of
p
onto
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{U}
respectively.
Also assume
v_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v^{\star}_{p})
.
Hence,

d(p,v_{p})\leq d(p,v_{p}^{\star})+d(v_{p}^{\star},v_{p})\leq d(p,v_{p}^{\star})+d(v_{p}^{\star},u_{p})\leq d(p,v_{p}^{\star})+d(v_{p}^{\star},p)+d(p,u_{p})=d(p,\mathcal{U})+2\cdot d(p,\mathcal{V}^{\star}).

Now, assign each
p
to
v_{p}
in solution
\mathcal{V}
, which concludes

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}d(p,\mathcal{V})\leq\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}(d(p,\mathcal{U})+2\cdot d(p,\mathcal{V}^{\star}))=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+2\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\star},\mathcal{P}\right).

Hence,
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}^{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{P})\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+2\cdot\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
.

8.7
Proof of
Lemma

3.11

Assume
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
of size at most
k
is such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
.
Define

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
.
Obviously,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is a feasible solution for the
(k+s)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and since
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
contains
\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we conclude

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+s}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

9
Proof of
Lemma

2.3

Assume the number of well-separated pairs w.r.t.
(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{V})
is
k-m
for some
m\in[0,k]
.
We call a
u\in\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}

good
if

•

u
forms a well-separated pair with a
v\in\mathcal{V}
; and

•

v
is not the closest center to any inserted point in the epoch, i.e., all of the points assigned to
v
in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}
(denoted by
C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})
) are in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
.

Assume we have
g
many good centers in
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
and consider orderings
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}=\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\}
, such that for all
i\in[1,g]
, the center
u_{i}
is good and
v_{i}
is such that
(u_{i},v_{i})
is a well-separated pair.
Since
\left|\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right|\leq\ell+1
, there are at most
\ell+1
many centers
v
such that
C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})\not\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
.
So, we have at most
m+\ell+1
centers in
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
that are not good.

Now, assume
\mathcal{W}^{\star}=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{g},v_{g+1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
is derived by swapping at most
k-g\leq m+\ell+1
many centers in
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
.
According to
Lemma

3.9
for
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\star},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u_{i},C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})\right)+\sum_{i=g+1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g}3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})\right)+\sum_{i=g+1}^{k}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\right)

As a result, there exist a set of
k
centers
\mathcal{W}^{\star}
such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\star},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\right)\leq 3\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{final}}}\right)
and
|\mathcal{W}^{\star}\oplus\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}|\leq m+\ell+1
.
In order to complete the proof of lemma, it suffices to show
m\leq 4\cdot(\ell+1)
.

According to
Lemma

4.2
for
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
and
r=0
, there exist a
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
of size at most
k-\lfloor m/4\rfloor
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)\leq 6\gamma\cdot\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)\right)
.
By hypothesis we have
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)\leq 18\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\gamma\cdot\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\gamma\cdot(1+18)\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 456000\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}},\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{{init}}}\right).

Finally, since we assumed that
\mathcal{U}_{\textnormal{{init}}}
is maximally
\ell
-stable, we conclude that
\lfloor m/4\rfloor\leq\ell
which shows
m\leq 4\cdot(\ell+1)
.

Part III
Full Version

10
Preliminaries

10.1
Problem Setting

Assume we have a ground metric space
\mathbf{P}
with distance function
d
together with a subset
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
which contains the current present weighted points.
In the
k
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}
, we want to find
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of size
k
(called centers) so as to minimize the objective function
\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)\cdot d(p,\mathcal{U})
, where
w(p)>0
is the weight of
p
and
d(p,\mathcal{U})=\min_{q\in\mathcal{U}}d(p,q)
is the distance of
p
to
\mathcal{U}
.
In the dynamic setting, the current space
\mathcal{P}
is changing.
Each update is either deleting a point of
\mathcal{P}
, or inserting a points of
\mathbf{P}-\mathcal{P}
into
\mathcal{P}
and assign a weight to it.
The aim is to maintain a set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of
k
centers that is a good approximation of the optimum
k
-median in the current space
\mathcal{P}
at each point in time.

We consider the
improper
dynamic
k
-median problem,
which means even if a point is not present in
\mathcal{P}
, we can pick it as a center in our main solution (i.e. open it as a center).
In order to have an algorithm for the
proper

k
-median problem that opens centers only present in the current set of points
\mathcal{P}
at any time, we use the following result of
[
BCG+24
]
.

Lemma 10.1

(Lemma 9.1 in
[
BCG+24
]
)
.

Given any fully dynamic
(k,p)
-clustering algorithm that maintains an
\alpha
-approximate
improper solution, we can maintain a
2\alpha
-approximate proper solution while incurring a
O(1)
factor
in the recourse and an additive
\tilde{O}(n)
factor in the update time.

In
[
BCG+24
]
, the authors achieved this result by providing a projection scheme that for every set of improper centers
\mathcal{U}
returns a proper subset of current set of points. We refer the reader to Section 9 of
[
BCG+24
]
to see the complete procedure.

10.2
Notations

By a simple scaling, we can assume that all of the distances in the metric space are between
1
and a parameter
\Delta
.
We use
\mathbf{P}
for the points of the ground metric space and
\mathcal{P}
for the set of current present points.
For simplicity, for each set
S
and element
p
, we denote
S\cup\{p\}
and
S\setminus\{p\}
by
S+p
and
S-p
respectively.
Each
p\in\mathcal{P}
has a weight denoted by
w(p)
and for every
S\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, we define

w(S):=\sum_{p\in S}w(p).

For two point sets
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we use
\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for their symmetric difference.
Note that if a point
p
is present in both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, but its weight is different in these two sets, we also consider
p\in\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
We assume the same for
\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
For each
S\subseteq\mathbf{P}
, we define
\pi_{S}:\mathbf{P}\rightarrow S
to be the projection function onto
S
. For each

p\in\mathbf{P}
and
S\subseteq\mathbf{P}
, we define
d(p,S):=d(p,\pi_{S}(p))
. For each
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, we use

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},S\right):=\sum\limits_{p\in S}w(p)\ d(p,\mathcal{U}).

We also define

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(\mathcal{U},S\right):=\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},S\right)}{w(S)}.

Assume
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
.
For any integer
m\geq 0
, we denote the cost of the optimum
m
-median solution for
\mathcal{P}
where we can only open centers from
\mathcal{C}
, by
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{m}^{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P})
, i.e.

\textnormal{{OPT}}^{\mathcal{C}}_{m}(\mathcal{P})=\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C},\\
|\mathcal{U}|\leq m\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right).

Whenever we do not use the superscript
\mathcal{C}
, we consider
\mathcal{C}
is the underlying ground metric space
\mathbf{P}
, i.e.

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{m}(\mathcal{P})=\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbf{P},\\
|\mathcal{U}|\leq m\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right).

Note that
\mathcal{U}
is a subset of the ground set
\mathbf{P}
, but its cost is computed w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.

For each
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
and
u\in\mathcal{U}
, we define

C_{u}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}):=\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(p)=u\}

to be the points of
\mathcal{P}
assigned to the center
u
in the solution
\mathcal{U}
(breaking ties arbitrarily).
For each point
p\in\mathbf{P}
and value
r\geq 0
, we define
\text{Ball}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r}(p)
to be the points in
\mathcal{P}
whose distances from
p
are at most
r
, i.e.

\text{Ball}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r}(p):=\{q\in\mathcal{P}\mid d(p,q)\leq r\}.

Note that
p
itself might not be in
\text{Ball}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r}(p)
since we only consider points of
\mathcal{P}
in this ball.

10.2.1
Constant Parameters

Throughout the paper we use constant parameters
\beta,\gamma
and
C
for convenience.

\beta=O(1)
is the constant approximation of the algorithm for static
k
-median on
\mathcal{P}
in
[
MP02
]
that runs in
\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{P}|\cdot k)
time.
The final values of
\gamma
and
C
are as follows.

\gamma=4000,\ \text{and}\ C=12\cdot 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}

10.3
Robustness

In this section we describe the notion of robustness.
This notion is first defined in
[
FLNS21
]
.
We change the definition in order to be able to get linear update time while the main good properties derived from the previous definition remain correct up to a constant overhead in approximation parts.

Definition 10.2

(
t
-robust sequence)
.

Assume
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
is a sequence of
t+1
points.
Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
and
B_{i}=\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p_{i})
for each
0\leq i\leq t
.
We call this sequence,
t
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
if for every
1\leq i\leq t
,

\displaystyle p_{i-1}=\begin{cases}p_{i}\quad&\text{if}\ \textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{i},B_{i}\right)\geq 10^{i}/5\\
q_{i}\quad&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}

where
q_{i}\in B_{i}+p_{i}
must satisfy

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(q_{i},B_{i}\right)\leq\min\{3\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B_{i}),\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},B_{i}\right)\}.

Note that in this definition, points
p_{i}
need not necessarily be inside
\mathcal{P}
.
But, the balls
B_{i}=\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p_{i})

are considered as a subset of current
\mathcal{P}
.
Also note that
q_{i}
is picked from
B_{i}+p_{i}
, but its cost is compared to the optimum improper solution for
1
-median problem in
B_{i}
, i.e.
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B_{i})
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
be a
t
-robust sequence and assume
B_{j}=\text{Ball}^{\mathcal{P}}_{10^{j}}(p_{j})
. Then, for every
1\leq j\leq t
, we have

d(p_{j-1},p_{j})\leq 10^{j}/2,\quad B_{j-1}\subseteq B_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad d(p_{0},p_{j})\leq 10^{j}/2.

Proof.

The first part is trivial
if
\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)\geq 10^{j}/5
since
p_{j}=p_{j-1}
.
Now, assume
\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)<10^{j}/5
. By the definition of a
t
-robust sequence, we know

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle d(p_{j},p_{j-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in B_{j}}\frac{w(p)}{w(B_{j})}d(p_{j},p_{j-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in B_{j}}\frac{w(p)}{w(B_{j})}(d(p_{j},p)+d(p_{j-1},p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)+\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\cdot 10^{j}.

For the second part, if
p\in B_{j-1}
, we have

d(p,p_{j})\leq d(p,p_{j-1})+d(p_{j-1},p_{j})\leq 10^{j-1}+\frac{2}{5}\cdot 10^{j}\leq 10^{j},

which implies
B_{j-1}\subseteq B_{j}
.
For the last part,

d(p_{0},p_{j})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}d(p_{i-1},p_{i})\leq\frac{2}{5}\sum_{i=1}^{j}10^{i}=\frac{2}{5}\cdot\frac{10^{j+1}-2}{9}\leq 10^{j}/2.

∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
be a
t
-robust sequence and assume
B_{j}=\text{Ball}^{\mathcal{P}}_{10^{j}}(p_{j})
. Then, for every
0\leq i\leq t
and every
S\subseteq\mathcal{P}
containing
B_{i}
(i.e.
B_{i}\subseteq S
), we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},S\right)

Proof.

Since
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
is
t
-robust, for every
1\leq j\leq t
, we know

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j},B_{j}\right)\geq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{j-1},B_{j}\right).

(43)

Assume
q\in\mathcal{P}\setminus B_{j}
. Then, using
Lemma

10.3
,

d(q,p_{j})\geq 10^{j}\geq 2\ d(p_{0},p_{j}).

Hence,

\frac{3}{2}\ d(q,p_{j})=d(q,p_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\ d(q,p_{j})\geq d(q,p_{j})+d(p_{0},p_{j})\geq d(q,p_{0}),

which means

d(q,p_{j})\geq\frac{2}{3}\ d(q,p_{0}).

(44)

Finally, Since
B_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq B_{i}\subseteq S
(by
Lemma

10.3
) we can apply
Equation

43
and
Equation

44
repeatedly to get

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},S\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},B_{i}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i-1}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-1},B_{i}\setminus B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-2},B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},B_{i}\setminus B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{i-2},B_{i-1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{i-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},B_{1}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\setminus B_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},S\right).

∎

Definition 10.5

(
t
-robust point)
.

We say
p
is
t
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
whenever there exist a
t
-robust sequence
(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
such that
p_{0}=p
.

Definition 10.6

(robust solution)
.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a set of centers. We call it robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
, if for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
, the following condition holds.

•

u
is
t
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
, where
t
is the smallest integer satisfying

10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{U}-u)/200.

10.4
Well-Separated Pairs

Definition 10.7

(well-separated pair)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are two sets of centers.
For
u\in\mathcal{U}
and
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we call
(u,v)
a well-separated pair with respect to
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})
, whenever the following inequalities hold.

\displaystyle d(u,\mathcal{U}-u)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\gamma\ d(u,v)

\displaystyle d(v,\mathcal{V}-v)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\gamma\ d(u,v)

It is easy to see that each point
u\in\mathcal{U}
either forms a well-separated pair with a unique
v\in\mathcal{V}
, or it does not form a well-separated pair with any center in
\mathcal{V}
.
This can be shown by a simple argument using triangle inequality (assuming the value of
\gamma
is large enough).

10.5
Relation Between Improper and Proper Optimum Values

For some technical reasons, in some part of our algorithm, we look for proper solutions and for some parts we look for improper solutions. Here, we provide a relation between the cost of an improper and a proper solution, which we will use in our analyses.

Lemma 10.8
.

Assume
\mathbf{P}
is the underlying ground metric space and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
.
Then, we have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}^{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

Proof.

The left inequality is obvious since every proper solution can be considered as an improper solution as well.
For the right inequality, assume
\mathcal{V}^{*}
is the optimum improper solution for
k
-median on
\mathcal{P}
. Consider the projection function
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{V}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{V}^{*})
.
We show that
\mathcal{U}
is a
2
approximate solution for the proper
k
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}
.
Note that
\mathcal{U}
is a proper feasible solution.
Assume
v^{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
is projected to
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(v^{*})=v\in\mathcal{U}
. For each
p\in C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})
, we have

d(p,v)\leq d(p,v^{*})+d(v^{*},v)\leq 2\ d(p,v^{*}).

The last inequality is because
p\in\mathcal{P}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(v^{*})=v
.
By summing up this inequality for each
p\in C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})
(considering weights
w(p)
), we conclude

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v^{*},C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})\right).

Since
v\in\mathcal{U}
, this means

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v^{*},C_{v^{*}}(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P})\right).

Finally, by summing up these inequalities for each
v^{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
, we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{P}\right)=2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}),

which concludes the right inequality since

\textnormal{{OPT}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

∎

10.6
Key Lemmas Used Throughout The Paper

In this section, we provide the key lemmas that we use in the analysis of our algorithm.

Lemma 10.9

(Lazy Updates Lemma, Lemma 3.3 in
[
BCG+24
]
)
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
are two sets of points such that
|\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|\leq l
. Then for every
k\geq 0
we have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
of size at most
k
is such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
.
Define

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
.
Obviously,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is a feasible solution for the
(k+l)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and since
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
contains
\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we conclude

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

∎

Lemma 10.10

(Double-Sided Stability Lemma)
.

Assume for a point set
\mathcal{P}
and values
k
,
\eta
and
0\leq r\leq k
we have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-r}(\mathcal{P})\leq\eta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}).

Then, the following inequality holds.

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+\lfloor r/(12\eta)\rfloor}(\mathcal{P})

Proof.

Consider the LP relaxation for the
k
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}
for each
k
as follows.

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}(w(p)\cdot d(c,p))x_{cp}

s.t.

\displaystyle\quad x_{cp}\leq y_{c}

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathcal{C},p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}x_{cp}\geq 1

\displaystyle\forall p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}y_{c}\leq k

\displaystyle x_{cp},y_{c}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathcal{C},p\in\mathcal{P}

Note that
\mathcal{C}
is the set of potential centers to open. We assume
\mathcal{C}
is the underlying ground set of points
\mathbf{P}
.
This is because we considered the definition of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
for the improper case.

Denote the cost of the optimal fractional solution for this LP by
\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}
.
Since the space
\mathcal{P}
is fixed here, we denote
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
by
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
.
It is known that the integrality gap of this relaxation is at most
3

[
CS11
]
. So, for every
k
we have

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}.

(45)

Claim 10.11
.

For every
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
and
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 1
such that
\alpha+\beta=1
, we have

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}\leq\alpha\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}.

Proof.

Assume optimal fractional solutions
(x^{*}_{1},y^{*}_{1})
and
(x^{*}_{2},y^{*}_{2})
for above LP relaxation of
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
-median problems respectively. It is easy to verify that
(\alpha x^{*}_{1}+\beta x^{*}_{2},\alpha y^{*}_{1}+\beta y^{*}_{2})
is a feasible solution for fractional
(\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2})
-median problem whose cost is
\alpha\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}
, which concludes the claim.
∎

Now, plug
k_{1}=k-r
,
k_{2}=k+r/(12\eta)
,
\alpha=1/(12\eta)
and
\beta=1-\alpha
in the claim. We have

\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}=\frac{1}{12\eta}(k-r)+\left(1-\frac{1}{12\eta}\right)\left(k+\frac{r}{12\eta}\right)=k-\frac{r}{(12\eta)^{2}}\leq k.

As a result,

\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k}\leq\textnormal{{FOPT}}_{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}\leq\alpha\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{1}}+\beta\ \textnormal{{FOPT}}_{k_{2}}.

Together with
Equation

45
, we have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\alpha\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{1}}+3\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}.

We also have the assumption that
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{1}}=\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-r}\leq\eta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
, which implies

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq 3\alpha\eta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}+3\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}.

Finally

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}\leq\left(\frac{3\beta}{1-3\alpha\eta}\right)\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k_{2}}\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+\lfloor r/(12\eta)\rfloor}.

The second inequality follows from

\frac{3\beta}{1-3\alpha\eta}\leq\frac{3}{1-3\alpha\eta}=\frac{3}{1-1/4}=4.

∎

Lemma 10.12

(variation of Lemma 7.4 in
[
FLNS21
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are two set of centers and
\mathcal{P}
is a set of points such that
\mathcal{U}
is robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
. Then, for every well-separated pair
(u,v)
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})
, we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq 5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})\right).

Proof.

Since, the point set
\mathcal{P}
is fixed, denote
C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})
by
C_{v}
for simplicity.
If
d(u,v)=0
, the statement is trivial.
Now assume
d(u,v)\geq 1
.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is robust, then
u
is
t
-robust where
t
is the smallest integer such that

10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{U}-u)/200\geq(\gamma/200)\ d(u,v)=20\ d(u,v)

(46)

The second inequality is because
(u,v)
is well-separated pair and the equality is because
\gamma=4000
.
Assume
t^{*}
is the integer satisfying

20\ d(u,v)\leq 10^{t^{*}}<10\cdot 20\ d(u,v).

(47)

Note that
t^{*}\geq 1
. Since
t^{*}\leq t
and
u
is
t
-robust, then it is also
t^{*}
-robust which means there is a
t^{*}
-robust sequence

(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{t^{*}})
such that
p_{0}=u
.
For Simplicity, assume
B_{i}=\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p_{i})
for
0\leq i\leq t^{*}
.

Claim 10.13
.

For each
0\leq i\leq t^{*}
, we have
B_{i}\subseteq C_{v}
.

Proof.

Assume
q\in B_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle d(q,v)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(q,p_{i})+d(p_{i},u)+d(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(q,p_{i})+d(p_{i},p_{0})+d(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{i}+10^{i}/2+10^{t^{*}}/20

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot 10^{t^{*}}.

For the second inequality, we used
q\in B_{i}
,
Lemma

10.3
and
Equation

47
.
Now, for every
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}-v
we have

d(v,v^{\prime})\geq d(v,\mathcal{V}-v)\geq 4\cdot 10^{3}\ d(u,v)>10^{t^{*}+1}\geq 2\ d(q,v).

The second inequality is by
(u,v)
being well-separated (and
\gamma=4000
), the third one is by
Equation

47
.
This concludes
d(q,v^{\prime})\geq d(v,v^{\prime})-d(q,v)>d(q,v)
which means
v
is the closest center to
q
in
\mathcal{V}
. So,
q\in B_{i}
and finally
B_{i}\subseteq C_{v}
.
∎

Now, we according to the definition of
t
-robust sequence we have two cases.

Case 1:

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq 10^{t^{*}}/5
. In this case we have
p_{t^{*}-1}=p_{t^{*}}
and

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(v,p_{0})+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(v,u)+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{t^{*}}/20+10^{t^{*}-1}/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 10^{t^{*}}/10.

The second inequality holds by
Lemma

10.3
and
Equation

47
.
So,

d(v,p_{t^{*}})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10.

(48)

For every
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
we have

d(q,v)\geq d(p_{t^{*}},q)-d(v,p_{t^{*}})\geq 10^{t^{*}}-d(v,p_{t^{*}})\geq d(v,p_{t^{*}}).

The second inequality follows by
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
and the last inequality follows by

Equation

48
.
So,

d(q,v)\geq(d(q,v)+d(v,p_{t^{*}}))/2\geq d(q,p_{t^{*}})/2,

which implies

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}.

(49)

We also have

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)\geq 10^{t^{*}}/5\geq 2\ d(v,p_{t^{*}}),

(50)

by the assumption of this case and
Equation

48
.
Now, we conclude

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(B_{t^{*}})\cdot\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle w(B_{t^{*}})\cdot(\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)-d(v,p_{t^{*}}))+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle w(B_{t^{*}})\cdot\frac{\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}+\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}\right)}{2}.

The first inequality is triangle inequality, the second inequality holds by
Equation

50
and the last inequality holds by
Equation

49
.
Finally, since
B_{t^{*}}\subseteq C_{v}
(by
Claim

10.13
), we can apply
Lemma

10.4
to get

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)=3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right).

Case 2:

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(p_{t^{*}},B_{t^{*}}\right)<10^{t^{*}}/5
. In this case, for every
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
,

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\geq d(q,p_{t^{*}})-d(p_{t^{*}},p_{t^{*}-1})\geq 10^{t^{*}}-10^{t^{*}}/2=10^{t^{*}}/2.

The second inequality holds by
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
and
Lemma

10.3
.
So,

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\geq 10^{t^{*}}/2.

(51)

We also have

\displaystyle d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(v,u)+d(u,p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(v,u)+d(p_{0},p_{t^{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{t^{*}}/20+10^{t^{*}-1}/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 10^{t^{*}}/10.

The second inequality holds by
Equation

47
and
Lemma

10.3
.
So,

d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10.

(52)

Combining
Equation

51
and
Equation

52
, we have

d(v,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 10^{t^{*}}/10=\frac{1}{5}\cdot 10^{t^{*}}/2\leq\frac{1}{5}\cdot d(q,p_{t^{*}-1}),

which concludes

\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,v)}\leq\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})-d(p_{t^{*}-1},v)}\leq\frac{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}{d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})-\frac{1}{5}\cdot d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})}=5/4\leq 3.

So, we have

d(q,p_{t^{*}-1})\leq 3\ d(q,v).

Using this inequality for all
q\in C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}
, we conclude

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)\leq 3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right).

(53)

Hence,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B_{t^{*}})+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right).

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,B_{t^{*}}\right)+3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}-B_{t^{*}}\right).

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right).

The equalities hold since
B_{t^{*}}\subseteq C_{v}
(by
Claim

10.13
), the first inequality holds by the definition of
t^{*}
-robust sequence, the second inequality holds since
v\in B_{t^{*}}
(by
Equation

47
) and the last inequality holds by
Equation

53
.
Finally, applying
Lemma

10.4
implies

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{0},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(p_{t^{*}-1},C_{v}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot 3\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)\leq 5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right).

In both cases, we showed that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u,C_{v}\right)\leq 5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v,C_{v}\right)
which completes the proof.

∎

Lemma 10.14

(generalization of Lemma 7.3 in
[
FLNS21
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a set of points,
\mathcal{U}
is a set of
k
centers and
\mathcal{V}
is a set of at most
k+r
centers.
If the number of well-separated pairs with respect to
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})
is
k-m
, then there exist a
\bar{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
of size at most
k-\lfloor(m-r)/4\rfloor
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\bar{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq 6\gamma\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right).

Proof.

Consider the standard LP relaxation for the weighted
k
-median problem. We consider the set of potential centers to open
\mathcal{U}
, and we want to open at most
k-(m-r)/4
many centers. So, we have the following LP.

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}(w(p)\cdot d(u,p))x_{up}

s.t.

\displaystyle\quad x_{up}\leq y_{u}

\displaystyle\forall u\in\mathcal{U},p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}x_{up}\geq 1

\displaystyle\forall p\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}y_{u}\leq k-(m-r)/4

\displaystyle x_{up},y_{u}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall u\in\mathcal{U},p\in\mathcal{P}

Now, we explain how to construct a fractional solution for this LP.

Fractional Opening of Centers.

Consider the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
function.
Assume
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{I}+\mathcal{U}_{F}
is a partition of
\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}_{I}
contains those centers
u\in\mathcal{U}
satisfying at least one of the following conditions:

•

u
forms a well-separated pair with one center in
\mathcal{V}
.

•

|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq 2
.

For every
u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}
, set
y_{u}=1
and for every
u\in\mathcal{U}_{F}
set
y_{u}=1/2
.
First, we show that

\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}y_{u}\leq k-(m-r)/4.

Each center
u\in\mathcal{U}
that forms a well-separated pair with a center
v\in\mathcal{V}
has
|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u)|\geq 1
since
u
must be the closest center to
v
in
\mathcal{U}
.
Since the number of well-separated pairs is
k-m
, we have

k+r\geq|\mathcal{V}|=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}|\pi^{-1}(u)|\geq\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}}|\pi^{-1}(u)|\geq k-m+2\cdot\left(|\mathcal{U}_{I}|-(k-m)\right).

Hence,

|\mathcal{U}_{I}|\leq\frac{k+r+(k-m)}{2}=k-\frac{m-r}{2}.

Finally, we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}y_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{I}}y_{u}+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{F}}y_{u}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k-\frac{m-r}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{m-r}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k-\frac{m-r}{4}.

Fractional Assignment of Points.

For every
p\in\mathcal{P}
, assume
v_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{V}}(p)
is the closest center to
p
in
\mathcal{V}
and
u_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})
is the closest center in
\mathcal{U}
to
v_{p}
.
We have three cases:

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1
, then set
x_{u_{p}p}=1
. The cost of this assignment would be
w(p)\cdot d(u_{p},p)
.

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
and there is a center
u^{\prime}_{p}\in\mathcal{U}-u_{p}
such that
d(u_{p},u^{\prime}_{p})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
, then set
x_{u_{p}p}=x_{u_{p}^{\prime}p}=1/2
. Note that
u_{p}^{\prime}\neq u_{p}
which means point
p
is assigned one unit to centers.
The cost of this assignment would be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u^{\prime}_{p},u_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+\frac{\gamma}{2}\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})\right).

•

If
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
and the previous case does not hold, then since
(u_{p},v_{p})
is not a well-separated pair, there is a center
v^{\prime}_{p}\in\mathcal{V}-v_{p}
such that
d(v_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
. Let
u_{p}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v^{\prime}_{p})
and set
x_{u_{p}p}=x_{u^{\prime}_{p}p}=1/2
. First, we show that
u^{\prime}_{p}\neq u_{p}
. Since
y_{u_{p}}=1/2
, we have
u_{p}\in\mathcal{U}_{F}
which concludes
|\pi^{-1}_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{p})|\leq 1
.
We also know that
\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})=u_{p}
.
So,
v_{p}
is the only center in
\mathcal{V}
mapped to
u_{p}
which implies

\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v^{\prime}_{p})\neq u_{p}
or
u_{p}^{\prime}\neq u_{p}
(note that
v_{p}^{\prime}\neq v_{p}
). So, point
p
is assigned one unit to centers.
The cost of this assignment would be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u^{\prime}_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})+d(v^{\prime}_{p},u^{\prime}_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v^{\prime}_{p})+d(v^{\prime}_{p},u_{p})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+d(u_{p},v_{p})+d(v_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})\right)
