\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\ d(u_{p},v_{p})\right).

The second inequality is because of the choice of
u^{\prime}_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p}^{\prime})
and the last inequality is because
d(v_{p},v_{p}^{\prime})\leq\gamma\cdot d(u_{p},v_{p})
.

Bounding the Cost.

Assume
u_{p}^{*}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(p)
for each
p\in\mathcal{P}
.
We have

d(u_{p},v_{p})\leq d(u_{p}^{*},v_{p})\leq d(u_{p}^{*},p)+d(p,v_{p}).

The first inequality is by the choice of
u_{p}=\pi_{\mathcal{U}}(v_{p})
.
As a result,

\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)\ d(u_{p},v_{p})\leq\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)\left(d(u^{*}_{p},p)+d(p,v_{p})\right)=\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right).

(54)

In each of the cases of fractional assignments of points to centers, the cost of assigning a point
p\in C_{v}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P})
is at most

w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\ d(u_{p},v_{p})\right)
.
As a result, the total cost of this assignment is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in P}w(p)\left(d(p,u_{p})+(\gamma+1)\ d(u_{p},v_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)\left(d(p,v_{p})+(\gamma+2)\ d(u_{p},v_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)+(\gamma+2)\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\gamma\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right).

The second inequality follows by
Equation

54
.

Finally, note that the integrality gap of the LP relaxation is known to be at most
3

[
CS11
]
. As a result, there exist an integral solution whose cost is at most
6\gamma\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}\right)\right)

and this solution opens at most

k-\lfloor\frac{m-r}{4}\rfloor
centers which completes the proof.

∎

11
Useful Subroutines of Our Algorithm

In this section, we provide useful static subroutines that we are going to use in our main algorithm.

11.1
Rand-Local-Search

This algorithm is first introduced in
[
BCG+24
]
.
Given a set of points
\mathcal{P}
, a set of
k
centers
\mathcal{U}
and a number
s\leq k
, the aim is to find a good subset of centers
\mathcal{U}
of size
k-s
.
The algorithm starts with an arbitrary
\mathcal{U}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
of size
k-s
and tries to improve this subset by random local swaps for
\tilde{\Theta}(s)
many iterations.

Algorithm 6

(Algorithm 3 in
[
BCG+24
]
),
Rand-Local-Search
(
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{U},s
)

1

\mathcal{U}^{*}\leftarrow
arbitrary subset of
\mathcal{U}
of size
k-s
.

2

for

\tilde{\Theta}(s)
iterations

do

3

Sample
v\in\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{U}^{*}
independently and uniformly at random.

4

z^{*}\leftarrow\arg\min_{z\in\mathcal{U}^{*}+v}\{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{*}+v-z,\mathcal{P}\right)-\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{*},\mathcal{P}\right)\}
.

5

\mathcal{U}^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}^{*}-z^{*}+v
.

6

return

\mathcal{U}^{*}
.

We have the following lemma which shows the subset derived from
Rand-Local-Search
is actually a good subset w.h.p.

Lemma 11.1

(Lemma 3.18 in
[
BCG+24
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{U}^{*}
is returned by
Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{U},s)
, then with high probability,

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{*},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-s}(\mathcal{P}).

11.2
Develop-Centers

Given a set of points
\mathcal{P}
, a set of centers
\mathcal{U}
and an integer
s\geq 1
,
the aim of this subroutine is to extend
\mathcal{U}
to
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
by adding
s
centers which is a good approximation to the best solution that adds
s
centers to
\mathcal{U}
, i.e. the algorithm finds
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq\beta\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}\right).

The algorithm defines a new metric space and runs a standard
(s+1)
-median static algorithm on this space.
The high level idea behind this subroutine is that we have a set of fixed
k
centers
\mathcal{U}
that must be contained in
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
.
So, if we can treat these fixed centers as a single center instead of a set of
k
centers, we might be able to reduce the problem to
(s+1)
-median.
So, we contract all of the points
\mathcal{U}
to a single point
u^{*}
and define a new space
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{*}
with a new metric
d^{\prime}
as follows.

\begin{cases}d^{\prime}(x,u^{*}):=d(x,\mathcal{U})&\forall x\in\mathcal{P}\\
d^{\prime}(x,y):=\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*}),d(x,y)\}&\forall x,y\in\mathcal{P}\end{cases}

(55)

We define the weight of a point
x\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}-u^{*}
the same weight of
x
in
\mathcal{P}
.
Finally, we set
w(u^{*})
to be very large say
w(u^{*})=\beta nW\Delta
, where
W
is the maximum weight of points in
\mathcal{P}
.
This makes any
\beta
approximate solution for
(s+1)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to contain
u^{*}
by force.

Claim 11.2
.

(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is a metric space.

Proof.

Intuitively, we can derive
d^{\prime}
by the metric of shortest path in the following graph.
For each
p\in\mathcal{P}
we have a node.
The weight of the edge
pq
for
p,q\in\mathcal{U}
is
0
and the weight of any other two point is their
d
distance.
Now,
d^{\prime}
exactly equals to the metric derived by the shortest in this graph, which is well-known to be a metric.
By the way, we provide another proof here that does not depend in this fact and is self-contained.

Now, we proceed with the formal proof.
The only non trivial property about
d^{\prime}
is the triangle inequality. We need to show that for every
x,y,z\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we have

d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(y,z).

If at least two of
x,y
and
z
are equal, the inequality is trivial. Otherwise, by symmetry between
x
and
y
, we have one of the following three cases.

Case 1:

z=u^{*}
and
x,y\neq u^{*}
.

This case is trivial by the definition of
d^{\prime}
since

d^{\prime}(x,y)=\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*}),d(x,y)\}\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*})=d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(y,z).

Case 2:

y=u^{*}
and
x,z\neq u^{*}
.

Assume
u_{x},u_{z}\in\mathcal{U}
are the projection of
x
and
z
on
\mathcal{U}
respectively with respect to metric
d
. So,

d(x,\mathcal{U})=d(x,u_{x})
,
d(z,\mathcal{U})=d(z,u_{z})
and
d(x,u_{x})\leq d(x,u_{z})
.
Note that

d^{\prime}(x,y)=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})=d(x,\mathcal{U})=d(x,u_{x})
and

d^{\prime}(z,y)=d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})=d(z,\mathcal{U})=d(z,u_{z})
.
As a result,

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(x,u_{x})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(x,u_{z})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(x,z)+d(z,u_{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(x,z)+d^{\prime}(z,y).

The first inequality is because of the choice of
u_{x}
in
\mathcal{U}
and the second inequality is triangle inequality for metric
d
.
So, we have the following

d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d^{\prime}(z,y).

(56)

Since
d^{\prime}
is non negative, we also have

d^{\prime}(x,y)=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq\left(d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})\right)+d^{\prime}(z,y)

(57)

Combining
Equation

56
and
Equation

57
we have

d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*}),d(x,z)\}+d^{\prime}(z,y)=d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(z,y).

Case 3:

x,y,z\neq u^{*}
.

According to the definition of
d^{\prime}
we have one of the following sub cases.

•

d^{\prime}(y,z)=d^{\prime}(y,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})
.

By the second case that we have already proved, we know that

d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*}).

Hence,

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*}),d(x,y)\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(y,z).

•

d^{\prime}(x,z)=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})
.

By symmetry between
x
and
y
, this sub case is similar to the previous sub case.

•

d^{\prime}(y,z)\neq d^{\prime}(y,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})
and
d^{\prime}(x,z)\neq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(z,u^{*})
.

According to the definition of
d^{\prime}(x,z)
and
d^{\prime}(y,z)
we have

d^{\prime}(x,z)=d(x,z)
and
d^{\prime}(y,z)=d(y,z)
.
Then, be triangle inequality for
d
we have

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(y,u^{*}),d(x,y)\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(x,z)+d(y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(y,z).

In all cases, we proved
d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq d^{\prime}(x,z)+d^{\prime}(y,z)
which concludes
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is a metric space.
∎

Next, we run any algorithm for
(s+1)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
w.r.t. metric
d^{\prime}
to find a
\mathcal{F}
of size at most
(s+1)
which is a
\beta
approximation for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{s+1}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
.
Finally, we let
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}=\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{F}-u^{*})
.
Note that all of the points of
\mathcal{F}-u^{*}
are present in
\mathcal{P}
and also the size of
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
is at most
k+s
since
u^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Algorithm 7

Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{U},s)

1

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{*}
.

2

w(u^{*})\leftarrow\beta nW\Delta
.

//
W
is maximum weight of points in
\mathcal{P}
and
\beta
is defined in
Section

10.2.1

3

Consider
D^{\prime}
as an oracle to the metric
d^{\prime}
defined in
Equation

55
.

4

\mathcal{F}\leftarrow
any
\beta
approximate solution for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{s+1}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
with access to distance oracle
D^{\prime}
.

5

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}+(\mathcal{F}-u^{*})
.

6

return

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
.

It is not obvious how the cost of a set w.r.t. metrics
d
and
d^{\prime}
relate to each other. But, we will show that solution
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t. metric
d^{\prime}
is going to be a good solution for our purpose w.r.t. metric
d
as well.
So, we show the following guarantee for this subroutine.

Lemma 11.3
.

If
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
is returned by
Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{U},s)
, then we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq\beta\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}\right).

Proof.

First, we show how the objective function of two solutions with respect to metrics
d
and
d^{\prime}
relate to each other.
Denote
\textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(X,\mathcal{P}\right)
to be the cost of
X
with respect to metric
d
on
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.
\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}w(p)d(p,X)
, and
\textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(X,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)
to be the cost of
X
with respect to metric
d^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, i.e.
\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}w(p)d^{\prime}(p,X)
.
The following claim relates metrics
d
and
d^{\prime}
.

Claim 11.4
.

Assume
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. Then, the following holds.

\textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{F}+u^{*},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right).

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{*}
. Since
d^{\prime}(u^{*},\mathcal{F}+u^{*})=0
and
d(u,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=0
for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
, it suffices to show that for each
x\in\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U}
, we have
d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})
.
Since

d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=\min\{d(x,\mathcal{F}),d(x,\mathcal{U})\}
, we have one of the following two cases.

Case 1:
d(x,\mathcal{U})\leq d(x,\mathcal{F})
.

This means
d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d(x,\mathcal{F})
. As a result, for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
we have

d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d(x,f)

(58)

We also have the following since
d^{\prime}
is non negative.

d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f,u^{*})

(59)

Combining
Equation

58
and
Equation

59
, for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f,u^{*}),d(x,f)\}=d^{\prime}(x,f).

Hence,
d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})
implying that

d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})=d(x,\mathcal{U})=d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U}).

The last equality is because of the assumption of this case.

Case 2:
d(x,\mathcal{U})>d(x,\mathcal{F})
.

Assume
f_{x}\in\mathcal{F}
is the projection of
x
onto
\mathcal{F}
with respect to metric
d
.
We are going to show that
f_{x}
is also the projection of
x
onto
\mathcal{F}+u^{*}
with respect to metric
d^{\prime}
.

We have
d(x,f_{x})=d(x,\mathcal{F})\leq d(x,\mathcal{U})=d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})
.
So,

d(x,f_{x})\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})

(60)

By non negativity of
d^{\prime}
, we have

d(x,f_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lem-costd'=costd-case2-eq1})}}{{\leq}}d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f_{x},u^{*}),

which concludes

d(x,f_{x})=\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f_{x},u^{*}),d(x,f_{x})\}=d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})
. So,

d(x,f_{x})=d^{\prime}(x,f_{x}).

(61)

Now, assume
f\in\mathcal{F}
is arbitrary. We have

d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lem-costd'=costd-case2-eq2})}}{{=}}d(x,f_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lem-costd'=costd-case2-eq1})}}{{\leq}}d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f,u^{*}),

(62)

as well as

d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lem-costd'=costd-case2-eq2})}}{{=}}d(x,f_{x})\leq d(x,f).

(63)

The inequality in
Equation

63
is because of the choice of
f_{x}
.
Combining
Equation

62
and
Equation

63
, we have

d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})\leq\min\{d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})+d^{\prime}(f,u^{*}),d(x,f)\}=d^{\prime}(x,f).

As a result, for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})\leq d^{\prime}(x,f)
. Combining with
d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})\leq d^{\prime}(x,u^{*})
(because of
Equation

60
and
Equation

61
), we conclude

d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})=d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})
. Hence,

d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=d(x,\mathcal{F})=d(x,f_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lem-costd'=costd-case2-eq2})}}{{=}}d^{\prime}(x,f_{x})=d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*}).

The first equality is because of the assumption of this case that
d(x,\mathcal{U})>d(x,\mathcal{F})
.

Objective Function:

In each case, we showed
d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})
which concludes the lemma as follows.

\displaystyle\textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{P}}w(x)\cdot d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U}}w(x)\cdot d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U}}w(x)\cdot d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x\in(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{U})+u^{*}}w(x)\cdot d^{\prime}(x,\mathcal{F}+u^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{F}+u^{*},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right).

Note that we used
d^{\prime}(u^{*},\mathcal{F}+u^{*})=0
and
d(x,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U})=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
.

∎

Now, assume
\mathcal{F}
is the
\beta
approximate solution found by
Develop-Centers
in line 4.
Let

\mathcal{F}^{*}=\arg\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\left\{\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)\right\}.

We have

\displaystyle\textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{F}+u^{*},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}^{d^{\prime}}_{s+1}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\beta\ \textsf{Cost}^{d^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{*}+u^{*},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\ \textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}^{*}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right),

where
\textnormal{{OPT}}^{d^{\prime}}_{s+1}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
is the optimum
(s+1)
-median w.r.t. metric
d^{\prime}
in space
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
The first and the last equalities follow from
Claim

11.4
.
The second equality follows from
u^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
(since
w(u^{*})
is too large and since
\mathcal{F}
is
\beta
-approximate it should contain this point).
The first inequality follows from the approximation ratio of solution
\mathcal{F}
.
The last inequality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}+u^{*}
is a set of size at most
s+1
.
Then we have the following as desired.

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}\right)=\textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)\leq\beta\ \textsf{Cost}^{d}\left(\mathcal{F}^{*}+\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}\right)=\beta\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}\right).

∎

11.3
Fast-One-Median

The aim of this algorithm is to solve the
1
-median problem on a set
B
as fast as possible.
In order to find a single center which is a
3
approximate of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B)
, we sample a set
S
of
\Theta(\log n)
points from
B
independently.
The sampling distribution is such that every
p\in B
is sampled proportional to its weight, i.e. with probability
w(p)/w(B)
.
Then, we find the best center between these sampled points.
So, we have the following algorithm.

Algorithm 8

Fast-One-Median
(B)

1

B\leftarrow\{b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{|B|}\}
an arbitrary ordering.

2

S\leftarrow\emptyset
.

3

for

\tilde{\Theta}(\log n)
iterations

do

4

r\leftarrow[0,1]
uniformly at random.

5

i^{*}\leftarrow
smallest index such that
\sum_{j=1}^{i^{*}}w(b_{j})\geq r\cdot w(B)
.

6

S\leftarrow S+b_{i^{*}}
.

7

q\leftarrow\arg\min\{s\in S\mid\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(s,B\right)\}
.

8

return

q
.

We show the following guarantee of this algorithm.

Lemma 11.5
.

If
q
is returned by
Fast-One-Median
(B)
, then with high probability,

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(q,B\right)\leq 3\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B).

Note that in this algorithm,
q
is a proper solution for
1
-median problem on
B
, but in this lemma
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B)
stands for the optimum improper solution.
So, the solution of the algorithm is compared with the best improper solution, although it returns a proper solution.

Proof.

Let
m=|B|
and
z^{*}
be the optimal solution to the improper
1
-median problem in
B
.
If
y
is sampled from the distribution where the probability of choosing
p
is
w(p)/w(B)
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(y,B\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in B}\frac{w(y)}{w(B)}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(y,B\right)=\sum_{y\in B}\frac{w(y)}{w(B)}\sum_{x\in B}w(x)\cdot d(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w(B)}\sum_{y\in B}\sum_{x\in B}w(y)w(x)\cdot d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w(B)}\sum_{y\in B}\sum_{x\in B}w(y)w(x)\cdot(d(x,z^{*})+d(z^{*},y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w(B)}\left[\sum_{y\in B}w(y)\cdot\sum_{x\in B}w(x)\cdot d(x,z^{*})+\sum_{x\in B}w(x)\cdot\sum_{y\in B}w(y)\cdot d(y,z^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in B}w(x)\cdot d(x,z^{*})+\sum_{y\in B}w(y)\cdot d(y,z^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B).

Thus, the expected approximation ratio of the
1
-median solution
y
is at most
2
. Hence, by Markov’s inequality, the probability that the approximation ratio of sample
y
is bigger than
3
, is at most
2/3
.
Finally, since
Fast-One-Median
samples a set
S
of
\Theta(\log n)
points from
B
independently,
the probability that all of these samples fail to have approximation ratio less than
3
is at most
(2/3)^{|S|}=1/n^{\Theta(1)}
.
As a result, with high probability there exists some
y\in S
such that
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(y,B\right)\leq 3\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B)
.

∎

12
Description of the Algorithm

In this section, we provide description of the algorithm as well as the main ideas and arguments on why the algorithm works correctly and how we achieve approximation, recourse and running time bounds.

The algorithm divides the input stream into some epochs on the fly.
At the beginning of each epoch we have a solution
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of
k
centers for the current space
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
The solution is
50\beta=O(1)
17
17

17

Recall
\beta
from
Section

10.2.1
approximate and is robust with respect to
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
Throughout the epoch we always maintain a
O(\beta)=O(1)
approximate solution.
Then, at the end of the epoch we build a solution using only the initial solution
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of this epoch.
So, this new solution is independent from the solutions maintained during the epoch.
This new solution is going to be
50\beta
approximate and robust with respect to the new space and this is going to be the starting point of the next epoch. Now, we explain the algorithm on each epoch.

At the beginning of the epoch we find a value
l
such that

\Omega(1)\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq O(1)\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Note that the value of
l
could be zero.
Next, we will find a subset of
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of size
k-l
like
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
whose cost is at most
O(1)\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
We call this part of the algorithm
Remove-Centers
.
So, after this part, we have
l
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of size
k-l
with above guarantees.

Next, for
l
many iterations, we do lazy updates on
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
as follows.
If a point gets inserted, we add it to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, and if a point gets deleted we do nothing (since we are dealing with the improper case, we can maintain a center in our main solution even if it gets deleted from the space).
Obviously, during these updates the size of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is at most
k
which means our solution is valid.
We will prove for
l
many iterations, this solution is a constant approximation for the value of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}

on the current space.
We call this part of the algorithm
Lazy-Updates
.

Then, we do one more update to get the final space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
in this epoch.
Now, we find a
10\beta
approximate solution
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
by adding
O(l+1)
many centers to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
. Note that the solution might have more than
k+l+1
centers, although our goal is to compete with
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
This is not obvious why such a solution exists.
But, we show that it is possible and actually, the subroutine
Develop-Centers
introduced in
Section

11.2
finds such a solution for this part.

Next, we add all of the inserted points during these
l+1
updates to
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
i.e.
\mathcal{V}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}+(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
This is because we want a good solution for the new set of points
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
and newly added points might be costly for
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
.
Note that if the weight of a point
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
is changed (i.e. it gets deleted and then inserted with another weight), we consider it in
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
Next, we reduce the size of
\mathcal{V}
to get a subset of
k
centers
\mathcal{W}
that is going to be a
32\beta
approximate solution for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.
We do this part by calling subroutine
Rand-Local-Search
introduced in
Section

11.1
.

So far, we have a solution
\mathcal{W}
for the new set of point
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
that satisfies
|\mathcal{W}\oplus\mathcal{U}^{(0)}|=O(l+1)
and is
32\beta
approximate for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.
Next, we make solution
\mathcal{W}
robust w.r.t. the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
and argue that it is actually a
50\beta
approximate solution.
We call this part of the algorithm
Robustify
.
Finally, we can start the next epoch with this new solution of
k
centers, since it is
50\beta
approximate and robust w.r.t. the new space.

So, we have the following pseudo-code for what we do in an epoch in a high level.
\mathcal{P}^{(l)}
in line 3 denotes the space after
l
updates in this epoch.

Algorithm 9

Main-Epoch
(
\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
)

1

\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},l\right)\leftarrow

Remove-Centers
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)})
.

2

Lazy-Updates
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},l)
.

3

\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{(l)}\pm p_{l+1}
.
//
read the
(l+1)^{th}
update.

4

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}\leftarrow

Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},(8C+2)(l+1))
.

//
C=12\cdot 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}
.

5

\mathcal{V}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}+(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.

6

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow

Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{V},|\mathcal{V}|-k)
.

7

\mathcal{U}^{(l+1)}\leftarrow

Robustify
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{W})
.

12.1
Remove-Centers

Assume

l^{*}=\arg\max\left\{0\leq l\leq k\;\middle|\;\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\right\}.

(64)

Note that
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
is considered in the improper case.
We will find a value
l^{\prime}
such that
l^{\prime}=\Omega(l^{*})
and

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

(65)

Let
\mathcal{R}=\{0,2^{0},2^{1},2^{2},\ldots,2^{\lfloor\log k\rfloor}\}
.
We iterate over
r\in\mathcal{R}
in increasing order starting from
0
, and call
Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},r)
introduced in
Section

11.1
.
If the cost of the subset returned by this subroutine is less than
14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)
, we take the next
r\in\mathcal{R}
and repeat the process.
We do this until reaching the maximum
r^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{k-r^{\prime}}=

Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},r^{\prime})
satisfies

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{k-r^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right).

Then, we let
l^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
(note that for simplicity, we have written this in the pseudo-code in a different way).
Later, we will show that this
l^{\prime}
satisfies
Equation

65
and
l^{\prime}\geq l^{*}/2
with high probability.
Next, we let
l=\lfloor l^{\prime}/C\rfloor
where
C
is
12\cdot 400\gamma\beta\alpha^{2}
and is determined using the Double-Sided Stability
Lemma

10.10
.
Note that
l+1=\Omega(l^{\prime}+1)=\Omega(l^{*}+1)
since
C
is a constant.
We will prove that for this value of
l
we have the following guarantee.

\frac{1}{3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Next, we find a subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of size
k-l
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

(66)

We do this by calling
Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},l)
.
So, we have the following algorithm for
Remove-Centers
.

Algorithm 10

Remove-Centers
(
\mathcal{U}
)

1

\mathcal{R}\leftarrow\{0,2^{0},2^{1},2^{2},\ldots,2^{\lfloor\log k\rfloor}\}
.

2

for

r\in\mathcal{R}

do

3

\mathcal{U}_{k-r}\leftarrow

Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},r)
.

4

if

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{k-r},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)>14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

then

5

break
for
loop.

6

l^{\prime}\leftarrow\lfloor r/2\rfloor
.

7

l\leftarrow\lfloor l^{\prime}/C\rfloor
where
C=12\cdot 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}
.

8

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\leftarrow

Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{U},l)
.

9

return

\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},l\right)
.

12.2
Lazy-Updates

So far, we have
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of size
k-l
.
Now we perform
l
many updates in the input stream as follows.
Whenever a point
p
gets inserted to space, we add it to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
and if a point
p
gets deleted, we keep
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
unchanged.
Note that since we deal with the improper dynamic
k
-median, we can maintain a center even if it gets deleted from the space.
In order to prove this solution is actually a constant approximation of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
, where
\mathcal{P}
is the current space during these lazy updates, we combine the guarantee in
Equation

66
and Lazy Updates
Lemma

10.9
.
This concludes our solution is always a constant approximation of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})
for the current space
\mathcal{P}
during the next
l
updates.

Algorithm 11

Lazy-Updates
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},l)

1

for

i=1
to
l

do

2

Read an update in the input stream.

3

if

this update is insertion of
p

then

4

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}^{\prime}+p
.

12.3
Develop-Centers

This part starts after the
(l+1)
’th update in the input stream.
So, we have the final set of points
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
We will add at most
(8C+2)(l+1)=O(l+1)
centers to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
to find a set
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

(67)

We do this by calling
Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},(8C+2)(l+1))
introduced in
Section

11.2
.
According to the guarantee of this subroutine, we get a
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq\beta\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq(8C+2)(l+1)\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right).

In order to show that this
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
actually satisfies
Equation

67
, it suffices to show that there exist
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
of size at most
(8C+2)(l+1)
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

We conclude this existential analysis by constructing
\mathcal{F}
using an optimum solution
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
for
(k+l+1)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
We show that it suffices to add
(8C+2)(l+1)
many centers of
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
to get a constant approximation of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}
.
These centers are elements of
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
that does not form well-separated pair with any center in
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
.

After termination of
Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},(8C+2)(l+1))
, we let

\mathcal{V}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}+(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
. Note that we also consider points whose weight is changed. This concludes that
\mathcal{V}
is a good set of centers in the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
as well as the initial space
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
The eventual conclusion is that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)\leq 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}).

Next, we reduce the size of
\mathcal{V}
to
k
in order to provide a feasible solution for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.
This part of the algorithm is done by calling
Rand-Local-Search
on
\mathcal{V}
to find a subset
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
of size
k
.
Since we know that the cost of
\mathcal{V}
on
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
is at most
10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
, with the guarantee of
Rand-Local-Search
, we conclude that the cost of
\mathcal{W}
on
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
is at most
32\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
with high probability.

12.4
Robustify

So far we have a solution
\mathcal{W}
which is
32\beta
approximate for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.
The aim of this part of the algorithm is to make
\mathcal{W}
, robust w.r.t. the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
Before we explain
Robustify
, we define an integer
t(u)
for every center
u
. Throughout the algorithm, if
\mathcal{U}
is our main solution, we always have an integer
t(u)
together with center
u
which indicates that
u
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t. the current space.
So, at the beginning of the epoch we know that every
u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.

Now, we explain how we make a robust solution w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
by changing
\mathcal{W}
.
Recall from
Definition

10.6
that
\mathcal{W}
is robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
, whenever for all
u\in\mathcal{W}
, we have the following condition

u\ \text{is}\ t\text{-robust w.r.t.}\ \mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\ \text{where}\ t\ \text{is the smallest integer satisfying}\ 10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{W}-u)/200.

(68)

In this subroutine, we have a set
\mathcal{S}
that helps us keep track of which elements violate Condition
68
.
First of all, we find some centers in
\mathcal{W}
that might not satisfy this condition and put them into set
\mathcal{S}
.
We do this by calling
Find-Suspects
.
Centers detected by
Find-Suspects
are going to be all centers
u\in\mathcal{W}
that
u
might not be
t(u)
-robust w.r.t. the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
, i.e. if a center
u\in\mathcal{W}
is not detected by
Find-Suspects
, then we are sure that
u
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
Note that these centers found by
Find-Suspects
are not necessarily all of the centers violating Condition
68
.

Then, we make all of the centers in
\mathcal{S}
robust, by calling
Empty-S
.
In this subroutine, for every
w\in\mathcal{S}
,
we determine the smallest integer
t
such that
10^{t}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/100

18
18

18

Note that the denominator is 100 (not 200 as in Condition
68
), this is because we want to prevent multiple calls to
Make-Robust
for a center. We use this in
Lemma

13.1
.

and replace
w
by a center
w_{0}
via a call to
Make-Robust
(w,t)
.
Now, this
w_{0}
satisfies Condition
68
.
We also remove
w
from
\mathcal{S}
, define
t(w_{0})=t
and save this integer with center
w_{0}
.

At this point, we are sure that every
u\in\mathcal{W}
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
So, in order to figure out if a center
u\in\mathcal{W}
violates Condition
68
, we make use of the integer
t(u)
.
If
t
is the smallest integer satisfying
10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{W}-u)/200
and
t\leq t(u)
, then
u
definitely satisfies Condition
68
.

Note that since
\mathcal{W}
is changing, for every
w\in\mathcal{W}
, the value of
d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/200
is changing as well.
As a result, after each change in
\mathcal{W}
, we should check whether or not any center in
\mathcal{W}
violates Condition
68
.
If such a center exists, we add it to
\mathcal{S}
.
We keep adding new elements to
\mathcal{S}
and removing elements from
\mathcal{S}
(by
Empty-S
), until all of the centers in the current
\mathcal{W}
satisfy Condition
68
and
\mathcal{S}
in turn is empty.
So, we have the following algorithm.

Algorithm 12

Robustify
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U}^{(0)})

1

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow

Find-Suspects
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U}^{(0)})
.

2

while

true

do

3

if

\mathcal{S}=\emptyset

then

4

return

5

Empty-S
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.

6

for

u\in\mathcal{W}

do

7

t\leftarrow\lceil\log\left(d(u,\mathcal{W}-u)/200\right)\rceil
.

8

if

t>t(u)

then

9

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}+u
.

It is not obvious why this algorithm terminates.
But, we show that
Robustify
calls
Make-Robust
on each center
w\in\mathcal{W}
at most once during a single call to
Robustify
.

Algorithm 13

Empty-S
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})

1

pick
w
arbitrarily from
\mathcal{S}
.

2

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}-w
.

3

t\leftarrow\lceil\log\left(d(w,\mathcal{W}-w)/100\right)\rceil
.

4

if

t\geq 0

then

5

w_{0}\leftarrow\text{{Make-Robust}}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},w,t)
.

6

else

7

w_{0}\leftarrow w
.

8

t\leftarrow 0
.

9

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}-w+w_{0}
.

10

t(w_{0})\leftarrow t
.

In order to analyse the recourse, we show that the number of points added to
\mathcal{S}
by calling
Find-Suspects
in the beginning of
Robustify
is at most
\tilde{O}(l+1)
. Then, we proceed by proving that the total recourse of the algorithm is
\tilde{O}(1)
in amortization.
Note that during a single call to
Robustify
the recourse might be as large as
k
, although we show the amortized recourse is
\tilde{O}(1)
throughout the total run of the algorithm (not just one epoch).

In order to analyse why the final solution is
50\beta
approximate, we show that the cost of the solution
\mathcal{W}
is not going to increase by more than a factor of
3/2
by calling
Robustify
.

As a result, we get the final solution of this epoch
\mathcal{U}
which is robust and is also
32\beta\times\frac{3}{2}\leq 50\beta
approximate for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})
.

12.4.1
Find-Suspects

In this subroutine, we find all centers
w\in\mathcal{W}
that might not be
t(w)
-robust w.r.t. the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
What we are looking for are the following centers.

•

New centers
u
added to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
(i.e.
u\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
).

•

Centers
u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{W}
whose neighborhood of radius
2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
has changed during this epoch (i.e.
\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}(u)\neq\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}}(u)
).

Note that in both cases, we also consider the weight of the points. For instance, there might be a point
p
which is present in both
\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}(u)
and
\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}}(u)
, but its weight has changed.
In this case, we consider center
u
satisfying the second condition.
So, we find all of the centers
u\in\mathcal{W}
satisfying at least one of the above conditions and add them to set
\mathcal{S}
.

Algorithm 14

Find-Suspects
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U}^{(0)})

1

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
.

2

for

p\in\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(0)}

do

3

for

u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{W}

do

4

if

d(u,p)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}

then

5

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}+u
.

6

return

\mathcal{S}
.

Note that in line 2, we also consider points
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
whose weight have changed.
According to the definition of
t(u)
, we know that
u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
We will show that if a center
u
is not detected by
Find-Suspects
, i.e. does not satisfy any of the two above conditions, then it remains
t(u)
-robust with respect to the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
This fact helps us argue that when the
Robustify
subroutine is terminated, the solution
\mathcal{U}=\textsc{Robustify}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U}^{(0)})
is robust.

12.4.2
Make-Robust

In this subroutine, we get a center
w
together with a non-negative integer
t
and return a center
w_{0}
, such that there exist a
t
-robust sequence like
(w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{t})
where
w_{t}=w
.
To do this, we simply use the definition of
t
-robust sequence.
Iterating over
i
from
t
to
1
, assume that we have found the point
w_{i}
(initially
w_{t}=w
).
First, we check whether

\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(w_{i},\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{i})\right)\geq 10^{i}/5.

If this inequality holds, we simply let
w_{i-1}=w_{i}
.
Otherwise, we find a
3
approximate solution
q_{i}
for
1
-median problem on
\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{i})
using
Fast-One-Median
algorithm introduced in
Section

11.3
.
Then, we let
w_{i-1}=q_{i}
if

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(q_{i},\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{i})\right)<\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{i},\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{i})\right).

Otherwise, we let
w_{i-1}=w_{i}
.
With guarantees of
Fast-One-Median
algorithm, we prove that we achieve a
t
-robust sequence at the end of
Make-Robust
. So, we have the following algorithm.

Algorithm 15

Make-Robust
(\mathcal{P},w,t)

1

w_{t}\leftarrow w
.

2

for

i=t
down to
1

do

3

B_{i}\leftarrow\text{Ball}_{10^{i}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{i})
.

4

z\leftarrow\textnormal{{AverageCost}}\left(w_{i},B_{i}\right)
.

5

if

z\geq 10^{i}/5

then

6

w_{i-1}\leftarrow w_{i}
.

7

else

8

q_{i}\leftarrow\text{{Fast-One-Median}}(B_{i},w_{i})
.

9

if

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{i},B_{i}\right)\leq\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(q_{i},B_{i}\right)

then

10

w_{i-1}\leftarrow w_{i}
.

11

else

12

w_{i-1}\leftarrow q_{i}
.

13

return

w_{0}
.

Remark 12.1
.

Instead of
Fast-One-Median
, we can use the algorithm of
[
MP02
]
to find a
\beta
-approximate solution
q_{i}
for
1
-median problem on
B_{i}
.
19
19

19

Recall
\beta
from
Section

10.2.1
.
But, if we do this, we lose another multiplicative factor of
\beta
on the approximation ratio in our final algorithm.
We can prevent it by performing this simple
Fast-One-Median
subroutine instead of
[
MP02
]
.

13
Correctness of the Algorithm

Assume we have
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
at the beginning of an epoch where
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is
50\beta
approximate and it is also robust.
The subroutine
Remove-Centers
, reduces the size of the solution to
k-l
, which means during the
Lazy-Updates
, we always maintain a solution of size at most
k
which is feasible.
The approximation guarantee of
Rand-Local-Search
in
Lemma

11.1
holds with high probability. But, since we call
Remove-Centers
at most
T
times during any
T
updates of input stream, we conclude that (by a simple union bound) with high probability all of these guarantees hold.

Next, the
(l+1)
’th update occurs.
Then, algorithm adds some centers to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and later reduces the size to
k
to get
\mathcal{W}
which is feasible for the new space
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.

13.1
Robustify
Calls
Make-Robust
Once for Each Center

First, we show that
Robustify
always terminates.

Lemma 13.1

(Lemma 6.1 in
[
FLNS21
]
)
.

If
Robustify
(\mathcal{W})
calls a
Make-Robust
for a center
w\in\mathcal{W}
and replaces it with
w_{0}
, then it will not call
Make-Robust
on
w_{0}
until the end of the
Robustify
subroutine.

Proof.

Assume a
Make-Robust
call to a center
w\in\mathcal{W}
is made and it is replaced by
w_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{1}
be the set of centers just before this call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w
.
At this point of time,
t(w_{0})
is the smallest integer satisfying
10^{t(w_{0})}\geq d(w,\mathcal{W}_{1}-w)/100
(note that
t(w_{0})\geq 0
, otherwise the algorithm should not have called
Make-Robust
duo to line 10 in the
Robustify
).
For the sake of contradiction, assume that another call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w_{0}
.
We assume that this pair
w
,
w_{0}
is the first pair for which this occurs.
Let
w^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{W}_{1}-w}(w)
which means
d(w,\mathcal{W}_{1}-w)=d(w,w^{\prime})
.
Then,

10^{t(w_{0})}\geq d(w,w^{\prime})/100
and
10^{t(w_{0})}<d(w,w^{\prime})/10
by definition of
t(w_{0})
. So, by
Lemma

10.3
, we have

d(w_{0},w)\leq 10^{t(w_{0})}/2\leq d(w,w^{\prime})/20.

(69)

Assume
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the set of centers just after the call to
Make-Robust
is made on
w_{0}
.
Since we assumed
w,w_{0}
is the first pair that another call to
Make-Robust
on
w_{0}
is made, we have one of the two following cases.

•

w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{2}
.
In this case, we have

d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})\leq d(w_{0},w^{\prime})\leq d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})\leq\frac{21}{20}\ d(w,w^{\prime})\leq 2\ d(w,w^{\prime}).

The last inequality holds by
Equation

69
.
This is in contradiction with the assumption that a call to
Make-Robust
on
w_{0}
is made, because
w_{0}
is
t(w_{0})
-robust where

10^{t(w_{0})}\geq d(w,w^{\prime})/100\geq d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})/200
. So,
w_{0}
must not be selected to be added to
\mathcal{S}
in
Robustify
.

•

There is
w^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{2}
that replaced
w^{\prime}
by a single call to
Make-Robust
.
When
Make-Robust
is called on
w^{\prime}
, the algorithm picks integer
t(w_{0}^{\prime})
that satisfies

10^{t(w_{0}^{\prime})}\leq d(w^{\prime},w_{0})/10
(otherwise, it should have picked
t(w_{0}^{\prime})-1
instead of
t(w_{0}^{\prime})
).
Hence,

d(w_{0}^{\prime},w^{\prime})\leq 10^{t(w_{0}^{\prime})}/2\leq d(w^{\prime},w_{0})/20\leq\left(d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})\right)/20\leq d(w,w^{\prime})/10.

(70)

The first inequality holds by
Lemma

10.3
and the last inequality is
Equation

69
.
As a result,

\displaystyle d(w_{0},\mathcal{W}_{2}-w_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(w_{0},w_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(w_{0},w)+d(w,w^{\prime})+d(w^{\prime},w_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(w,w^{\prime})/20+d(w,w^{\prime})+d(w,w^{\prime})/10

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{23}{20}\ d(w,w^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ d(w,w^{\prime}).

The last inequality holds by
Equation

69
and
Equation

70
.
This leads to the same contradiction.

∎

13.2
Correctness of
Robustify

Consider a call to
Robustify
occurs on
\mathcal{W}
.
First, in
Find-Suspects
, all of the centers
u\in\mathcal{W}
satisfying
u\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
or
d(p,u)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
for at least one
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
are added to
\mathcal{S}
.
We know that each center
u\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
at the beginning of epoch. We now prove the following.

Lemma 13.2
.

Assume
u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{S}
is returned by
Find-Suspects
. If
u\notin\mathcal{S}
, then
u
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
as well.

Proof.

Let
(p_{0},\ldots,p_{t(u)})
be a
t(u)
-robust sequence w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
, where
p_{0}=u
.
According to
Lemma

10.3
,

d(p_{0},p_{t(u)})\leq 10^{t(u)}/2
which concludes

\text{Ball}_{10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}(p_{t(u)})\subseteq\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}(u).

Because, the ball around
u
of radius
2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
is not changed (i.e.
\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}(u)=\text{Ball}_{2\cdot 10^{t(u)}}^{\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}}(u)
considering points by their weight), we conclude that ball around
p_{t(u)}
of radius
10^{t(u)}
is also not changed. Finally, with the definition of
t(u)
-robust sequence we conclude that
(p_{0},\ldots,p_{t(u)})
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
as well. So,
p_{0}=u
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
∎

Assume
\mathcal{U}=

Robustify
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U}^{(0)})
is the final solution of the algorithm for
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
According to above lemma and description of the algorithm, we show that
\mathcal{U}
is robust.

By
Lemma

13.1
we know that
Robustify
calls
Make-Robust
on each
u\in\mathcal{W}
at most once.
According to the proof of this lemma, if a center
u\in\mathcal{U}
is returned by a call to
Make-Robust
on center
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
, then
u
does not violate Condition
68
.
Now, assume no call to
Make-Robust
was made on
u\in\mathcal{W}
.
Hence,
u
was not found by
Find-Suspects
, and by
Lemma

13.2
, we conclude
u
is
t(u)
-robust w.r.t. the new set of points
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
Let
t
be the smallest integer such that

10^{t}\geq d(u,\mathcal{U}-u)/200
.
Since
u
was not added to
\mathcal{S}
, we conclude that
t(u)\geq t
(See Line
12
of
Robustify
).
So,
u
is
t
-robust w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
which means
u
does not violate Condition
68
for current
\mathcal{U}
.
As a result, the final
\mathcal{U}
returned by
Robustify
is robust.

Later, in
Section

15.2
, we show that it is also
50\beta
approximate.
This means that the final solution of the algorithm at the end of the epoch satisfies the conditions needed as the initial solution of the next epoch.

13.3
Correctness of
Make-Robust

Assume a call to
Make-Robust
on
(\mathcal{P},w,t)
.
In order to show that the returned value
w_{0}
is actually a
t
-robust point w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
, we check the definition of
t
-robust sequence.
According to the subroutine
Make-Robust
and the definition of
t
-robust sequence, we only need to show that for every
1\leq i\leq t
, the returned point
q_{i}
by
Fast-One-Median
(B_{i},w_{i})
satisfies
\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(q_{i},B_{i}\right)\leq 3\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{1}(B_{i}).

This is also the guarantee of
Fast-One-Median
in
Lemma

11.5
.

Later, in
Section

14
, we show that the total number of times that we call
Make-Robust
throughout the first
T
updates of the input stream is
\tilde{O}(T)
.
Hence, with high probability all of these independent calls to
Make-Robust
return correctly.

14
Recourse Analysis

In this section, we provide the recourse analysis of the algorithm. We show the following lemma.

Lemma 14.1
.

The Total recourse of the algorithm throughout the first
T
updates is at most

O(T(\log\Delta)^{2}).

14.1
Difference Between
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{W}

In
Develop-Centers
, we are adding at most
(8C+2)(l+1)
centers to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
to make
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
. Then, we add
\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
to get
\mathcal{V}
. As a result, the size of
\mathcal{V}
is at most
k+(8C+3)(l+1)
. Next, we reduce the size of this set to
k
by calling
Rand-Local-Search
on
\mathcal{V}
. Since both of
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{W}
are subsets of size
k
of set
\mathcal{V}
, we conclude that

|\mathcal{W}\oplus\mathcal{U}^{(0)}|\leq 2\cdot((8C+3)(l+1))\leq 20C(l+1).

So, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 14.2
.

For every epoch of length
l+1
, we have
|\mathcal{W}\oplus\mathcal{U}^{(0)}|\leq 20C(l+1)
.

14.2
Number of Centers Detected by
Find-Suspects

At the beginning of
Find-Suspects
, the algorithm sets
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
.
As a result, the size of this initial
\mathcal{S}
is at most
20C(l+1)
by
Lemma

14.2
.
Next, for every point
p
that has gone through an update (either insertion or deletion) in this epoch (i.e.
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
) the algorithm finds all centers
u\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
such that
d(u,p)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
.
We claim that for a fix
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
, there are at most
O(\log\Delta)
many centers
u
that satisfy this property.

Lemma 14.3
.

For each
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
, the number of
u\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
satisfying
d(p,u)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
is at most
O(\log\Delta)
.

Proof.

Assume
p\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}
.
Let
\mu
be the number of centers
u\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
that satisfy

d(p,u)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
.
For each of these centers like
u
, there is a
u^{\prime}
that was added to
\mathcal{S}
and a call to
Make-Robust
was made on
u^{\prime}
which returned
u
.
Assume
\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\mu}\}
is the set of centers
u
satisfying
d(p,u)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
ordered in decreasing order by the time they replaced
u_{i}^{\prime}
via a call to
Make-Robust
.
So, when
u_{i}
is added to the main solution, center
u_{i+1}
was already present in the main solution which concludes

10^{t(u_{i})}\leq d(u_{i},u_{i+1})/10,

by the definition of
t(u_{i})
at that time
for every
1\leq i\leq\mu-1
.
Hence,

10^{t(u_{i+1})}\geq d(p,u_{i+1})/2\geq(d(u_{i},u_{i+1})-d(p,u_{i}))/2\geq 5\cdot 10^{t(u_{i})}-10^{t(u_{i})}=4\cdot 10^{t(u_{i})}.

The first and last inequality holds by the assumption that
d(p,u)\leq 2\cdot 10^{t(u)}
for every
u\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{\mu}\}
.
Finally, this concludes

t(u_{\mu})>\cdots>t(u_{2})>t(u_{1})
. Since we assumed the distances between any two point in the space is between
1
and
\Delta
, we know
0\leq t(u_{i})\leq\lceil\log\Delta\rceil
for each
1\leq i\leq\mu
and so,
\mu\leq(\log\Delta)+2=O(\log\Delta)
.

∎

A quick corollary of this lemma is the following.

Corollary 14.4
.

The size of final
\mathcal{S}
returned by
Find-Suspects
in an epoch of length
l+1
is at most
O((l+1)\log\Delta)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

14.2
and
Lemma

14.3
, we have

|\mathcal{S}|\leq 10C(l+1)+|\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}|\cdot O(\log\Delta)=O((l+1)\log\Delta).

∎

14.3
Total Recourse

Assume the sequence of first
T
updates in the input stream. We show the total recourse of the algorithm during these
T
updates is at most
O(T(\log\Delta)^{2})
.
Consider that the algorithm performs
E
epochs in total.
For every
1\leq e\leq E
, let
l_{e}+1
,
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}
and
\mathcal{W}_{e}
be the length of, initial solution and the set of centers returned by
Rand-Local-Search
just before calling
Robustify
in epoch
e
respectively.
For every
1\leq e\leq E
and every
1\leq i\leq l_{e}+1
, let
\mathcal{U}^{(i)}_{e}
be the main solution of the algorithm after the
i
’th update in epoch
e
. As a result, the total recourse of the algorithm equals.

\sum_{e=1}^{E}\sum_{i=0}^{l_{e}}|\mathcal{U}^{(i+1)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(i)}_{e}|.

The recourse after each lazy update is at most
1
, which concludes for every
1\leq e\leq E
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{l_{e}}|\mathcal{U}^{(i+1)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(i)}_{e}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle l_{e}+|\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e})}_{e}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle l_{e}+|\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e})}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}|+|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2l_{e}+|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|.

Hence,

\sum_{e=1}^{E}\sum_{i=0}^{l_{e}}|\mathcal{U}^{(i+1)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(i)}_{e}|\leq\sum_{e=1}^{E}\left(2l_{e}+|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|\right)\leq 2T+\sum_{e=1}^{E}|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|.

So, it suffices to prove

\sum_{e=1}^{E}|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|=O(T(\log\Delta)^{2}).

Recall
Lemma

13.1
.
This lemma states that the
Robustify
calls a
Make-Robust
on every center
w\in\mathcal{W}
at most once.
So, if a center
u
is added to
\mathcal{S}
in
Robustify
, it is not going to be added to
\mathcal{S}
in this call to
Robustify
again.
According to the
Robustify
, it is obvious that
\sum_{e=1}^{E}|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|
is at most the number of times that elements are added to
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, we create a graph during the run of the algorithm as follows. The graph is a union of disjoint directed paths.
Each node of the graph is alive or dead.
At any time, every center in the main solution of the algorithm is corresponding to an alive node in this graph.
A center might be corresponding to several nodes in the graph (since it can be added to
\mathcal{S}
several times during the total run of the algorithm), but it is corresponding to only one alive node.

Initially, the graph consists of
k
alive nodes corresponding to the initial solution of
k
centers at the very beginning of the algorithm.
Now, we explain how we change the graph during the algorithm.
At the end of the epoch, we mark all of the nodes corresponding to centers
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}-\mathcal{W}
as dead.
Assume a center
u\in\mathcal{W}
at the end of an epoch.
We have the following cases.

•

u\notin\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
. In this case, we know that there is no alive node in the graph corresponding to
u
. We add a new alive node to the graph corresponding to
u
.
This would be the starting node of a new path in the graph.

•

u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and
u
is detected by
Find-Suspects
.
In this case, we know that
u
is corresponding to an alive node
V_{u}
in the graph.
We mark this node
V_{u}
dead and add a new alive node corresponding to
u
in the graph.
This is also the starting node of a new path in the graph.

•

u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and
u
is not detected by
Find-Suspects
, but
u
is added to
\mathcal{S}
during the
Robustify
.
In this case, we know that
u
is corresponding to an alive node
V_{u}
in the graph and also
u
is going to be replaced by
u_{0}
by a call to
Make-Robust
.
We mark
V_{u}
as dead, add a new node
V_{u_{0}}
corresponding to
u_{0}
in the graph and draw a directed edge from
V_{u}
to
V_{u_{0}}
.

•

u\in\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
and
u
is not detected by
Find-Suspects
, and
u
is not added to
\mathcal{S}
during the
Robustify
. In this case, we do not change the graph.

Figure 1:
The constructed graph looks like this. If a center is detected by
Find-Suspects
, it would be the end of the path. If a
Make-Robust
is called on a center, the path ending in the node corresponding to this center is extended.
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Find-Suspects
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
Make-Robust
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
dead
alive
alive

According to this construction of the graph, the total number of paths in the graph is equal to the total number of centers detected by
Find-Suspects
and the total number of nodes in the graph is equal to the total number of elements added to
\mathcal{S}
during the entire run of the algorithm.

By
Corollary

14.4
, the number of centers detected by
Find-Suspects
at the end of epoch
e
is at most
O((l_{e}+1)\log\Delta)
.
As a result, the total number of centers detected by
Find-Suspects
, during the run of the algorithm for the first
T
updates of the input stream is at most
O(T\log\Delta)
.
This concludes the number of paths of the graph is
O(T\log\Delta)
.

Now, we show that the length of each path in the graph is at most
O(\log\Delta)
. This completes the analysis.
Consider centers

u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{r}
corresponding to the directed path

V_{u_{1}},V_{u_{2}},\ldots,V_{u_{r}}

in this graph.
First, note that for every
1\leq i\leq r-1
, center
u_{i}
is not detected by
Find-Suspects
during the algorithm.
Otherwise, we would have marked
V_{u_{i}}
as dead and
V_{u_{i}}
would have been the end of the path.
The only reason that
u_{i}
is added to
\mathcal{S}
is that

10^{t(u_{i})}<d(u_{i},\mathcal{U}-u_{i})/200
.
In this case, the algorithm picks the smallest integer
t
satisfying
10^{t}\geq d(u_{i},\mathcal{U}-u_{i})/100
, replaces
u_{i}
with
u_{i+1}
by a call to
Make-Robust
(u_{i},t)
and sets
t(u_{i+1})=t
.
As a result, we have

10^{t(u_{i})}<d(u_{i},\mathcal{U}-u_{i})/200<d(u_{i},\mathcal{U}-u_{i})/100\leq 10^{t(u_{i+1})}

for every
1\leq i\leq r-1
.
Hence,

t(u_{1})<t(u_{2})<\cdots<t(u_{r})
.
Since, we assumed the maximum distance between any two points in the metric space is at most
\Delta
, we conclude that

t(u_{i})\leq\lceil\log\Delta\rceil
for every
1\leq i\leq r
.
Together with
t(u_{1})<t(u_{2})<\cdots<t(u_{r})
, we conclude that
r\leq\lceil\log\Delta\rceil+1=O(\log\Delta)
.

Putting everything together, the number of paths in the graph is at most
O(T\log\Delta)
and the length of each path is at most
O(\log\Delta)
, which concludes the total number of nodes in the created graph is at most
O(T(\log\Delta)^{2})
. Hence, the total number of elements added to
\mathcal{S}
during the run of the algorithm is at most
O(T(\log\Delta)^{2})
.
Finally, this implies

\sum_{e=1}^{E}|\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{e}-\mathcal{U}^{(l_{e}+1)}_{e}|=O(T(\log\Delta)^{2}),

which completes the analysis of recourse.

15
Approximation Ratio Analysis

In this section we prove that the approximation ratio of the algorithm is constant.

Important note:

Since, subroutines
Rand-Local-Search
and
Fast-One-Median
are randomised and their approximation guarantee hold with high probability, some of the lemmas in this section occur with high probability.
But, we omit repeating this phrase over and over and provide the statements without it.
Assume
T
many updates in the input stream.
According to the algorithm, we call
Rand-Local-Search
at most
O(T)
times.
According to the recourse analysis in
Section

14
, we call
Fast-One-Median
at most
\tilde{O}(T)
times.
As a result, we can conclude that all of these subroutines simultaneously return correctly with high probability.
So, we omit using w.h.p. in the analysis in this section.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
at the beginning of an epoch has approximation ratio
50\beta
. We will prove during the epoch, approximation ratio of our solution at any time is at most
2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}
.
Then, we prove that the approximation ratio of final
\mathcal{U}
at the end of epoch is
50\beta
which is the starting point of the next epoch.
Since the approximation ratio of the algorithm would be very large even if we optimize the parameters, we do not bother to use tight inequalities in the proofs.

15.1
Analysis of
Remove-Centers
and
Lazy-Updates

We start by analyzing
Remove-Centers
.
Assume
\mathcal{R}=\{0,2^{0},2^{1},2^{2},\ldots,2^{\lfloor\log k\rfloor}\}
and

l^{*}=\arg\max\left\{0\leq l\leq k\mid\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\right\}.

According to
Remove-Centers
, the value of
l^{\prime}
is the maximum number in
\mathcal{R}
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{k-l^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right).

Next, the algorithm sets
l=\lfloor l^{\prime}/C\rfloor
.
Now, we show good properties of this choice of
l
.

Lemma 15.1
.

We have

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

According to the definition of
l^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{k-l^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 50\beta\cdot 14\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

∎

Lemma 15.2
.

We have
l+1\geq(l^{*}+1)/(2C)
.

Proof.

First, we show that
l^{\prime}\geq l^{*}/2
.
We have three cases:

•

l^{*}=0
. The inequality is obvious.

•

l^{*}\geq 1
and
l^{\prime}=0
. In this case, the value of
l^{\prime\prime}=1\in\mathcal{R}
(which is bigger than
l^{\prime}
) also satisfies

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{k-l^{\prime\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{\prime\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{*}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+12\cdot 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(14\cdot 400\gamma\beta)\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right).

The first inequality holds by the guarantee of
Rand-Local-Search
in
Lemma

11.1
, the second one holds since
l^{\prime\prime}\geq l^{*}
, and the third one holds by the definition of
l^{*}
.
This is in contradiction with the choice of
l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
in the algorithm, since
l^{\prime\prime}>l^{\prime}
and the algorithm must have picked
l^{\prime\prime}
instead of
l^{\prime}
.

•

l^{*}\geq 1
and
l^{\prime}\geq 1
. In this case if we have
l^{\prime}<l^{*}/2
, then the above argument works for
l^{\prime\prime}=2l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
which leads to the same contradiction (note that since
l^{*}/2\leq k/2
, then
l^{\prime\prime}\leq k
must be in
\mathcal{R}
).

So, we have
l^{\prime}\geq l^{*}/2
.
Since both of
l^{\prime}
and
C
are integers, we have
l+1\geq l^{\prime}/C+1/C
. Hence,

l+1\geq(l^{\prime}+1)/C\geq(l^{*}+1)/(2C)
.

∎

Lemma 15.3
.

We have

\frac{1}{3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

Using Double-Sided Stability
Lemma

10.10
, we easily conclude that the algorithm picks an
l
with the property that both of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
and
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
are within a constant factor of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
as desired.
More precisely, let
\eta=3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}
and
r=l^{\prime}
in Double-Sided Stability
Lemma

10.10
.
Note that

\lfloor r/(12\eta)\rfloor=\lfloor l^{\prime}/(12\cdot 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2})\rfloor=\lfloor l^{\prime}/C\rfloor=l.

According to
Lemma

15.1
, we have
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-r}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq\eta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
Hence,

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+\lfloor r/(12\eta)\rfloor}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})=4\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Since
l^{\prime}\geq l
,
Lemma

15.1
concludes

\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

∎

Next, we bound the cost of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
returned by Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},l)
.

Lemma 15.4
.

We have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

According to
Lemma

11.1
and
Lemma

15.3
,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot(50\beta)\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})+12\cdot(3\cdot 10^{5}\gamma\beta^{2})\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\cdot 10^{6}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\cdot(4\cdot 10^{6}\gamma\beta^{2})\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

∎

Finally, we show that the maintained solution
during the lazy updates is a constant approximation of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}
on the current space.
Assume that
1\leq i\leq l
updates has happened to
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
and we have the current set of points
\mathcal{P}^{(i)}
.
According to the algorithm, the solution for this set of points is
\mathcal{U}^{(i)}
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}+(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{(i)}
.
Note that there might be some point
p
that has been added during these
i
updates and then deleted. So,
p
is not present in
\mathcal{P}^{(i)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
, but it is present in our solution
\mathcal{U}^{(i)}
. As a result,
Lazy-Updates

Lemma

10.9
and
Lemma

15.4
conclude

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(i)},\mathcal{P}^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}+(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}),\mathcal{P}^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}).

So, we have the following.

Corollary 15.5
.

The approximation ratio of algorithm during
Lazy-Updates
is at most
2\cdot 10^{7}\gamma\beta^{2}
.

15.2
Analysis of Develop-Centers

In this section, we show
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
returned by Develop-Centers is
10\beta
approximate for
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.

We start by proving that it is possible to add at most
O(l+1)
points to
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
to obtain a set of centers
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
which is
10
approximate of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
Then, we show
Develop-Centers
finds
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
that is
10\beta
approximate of
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
.
Assume
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
is the optimum
proper
solution of size
k+l+1
for
(k+l+1)
-median problem on
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
(we will use the assumption that this is a proper solution in
Lemma

15.8
).
Note that
\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
stands for the optimum improper solution.
According to
Lemma

10.8
,
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
is a
2
approximate solution for the improper
(k+l+1)
-median on
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
, i.e.

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

(71)

Lemma 15.6
.

If the number of well-separated pairs with respect to
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1})
is
k-m
, then there exists
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
of size at most
m+l+1
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

Consider orderings
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}=\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{k+l+1}^{*}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k+l+1}\}
such that
(u_{i},v_{i})
are all
k-m
well-separated pairs w.r.t.
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{V}_{k+l+1}^{*})
for
1\leq i\leq k-m
.
Define

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\{v_{k-m+1},v_{k-m+2},\ldots,v_{k+l+1}\}

of size
m+l+1
.
For simplicity, denote
C_{v_{i}}(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1},\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
by
C_{v_{i}}
for each
1\leq i\leq k+l+1
. Note that these sets give a partition of
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
Now, consider
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
We show how to assign points of
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
to centers in
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
For every
1\leq i\leq k-m
, we assign all of the points of
C_{v_{i}}
to
u_{i}
which is present in
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
This gives us,

\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},C_{v_{i}}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(u_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right).

(72)

The last inequality is by
Lemma

10.12
for
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
and every
1\leq i\leq k-m
.
For each
k-m+1\leq i\leq k+l+1
, we assign all of the points of
C_{v_{i}}
to
v_{i}
which is present in
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. This gives us,

\sum_{i=k-m+1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},C_{v_{i}}\right)\leq\sum_{i=k-m+1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right).

(73)

As a result,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},C_{v_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},C_{v_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=k-m+1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},C_{v_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=k-m+1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 5\ \sum_{i=1}^{k+l+1}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(v_{i},C_{v_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 5\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{k+l+1}^{*},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

The first inequality holds by
Equation

72
and
Equation

73
and the last inequality holds by
Equation

71
.
∎

Next, we show that the number of well-separated pairs w.r.t.
(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1})
is large.

Lemma 15.7
.

m\leq(8C+1)(l+1)
.

Proof.

Let
b=\lfloor(m-(l+1))/4\rfloor
.
By plugging
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
and
r=l+1
in
Lemma

10.14
, there exist a
\bar{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
of size at most
k-b
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\bar{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 6\gamma\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\right).

We also have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 50\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

and

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\leq 2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Combining these three inequalities together, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{{OPT}}_{k-b}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\bar{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\gamma\left(\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)+\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\gamma\left(50\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})+2\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(6\gamma)\cdot(52\beta)\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 400\gamma\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Now, with the maximality in the definition of
l^{*}
in
Equation

64
, we have
\left\lfloor\frac{m-(l+1)}{4}\right\rfloor=b\leq l^{*}
which concludes

m\leq 4(l^{*}+1)+(l+1).

Together with
Lemma

15.2
, we have

m\leq(8C+1)(l+1)
.
∎

Lemma 15.8
.

Let
s=(8C+2)(l+1)
. We have

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

According to
Lemma

15.6
, there exist a
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
of size at most
m+l+1
such that

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Note that
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
since
\mathcal{V}^{*}_{k+l+1}
is a proper solution.
We also have

|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}|\leq m+l+1\leq(8C+2)(l+1)=s,

where the last inequality holds by
Lemma

15.7
.
This concludes the lemma.
∎

Lemma 15.9
.

Assume
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}
is returned by
Develop-Centers
(\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\mathcal{U}^{(0)},s)
.
Then,

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

Proof.

According to
Lemma

15.8
and the guarantee of
Develop-Centers
in
Lemma

11.3
, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq\beta\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{(0)},\\
|\mathcal{F}|\leq s\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}+\mathcal{F},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)\leq 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)}).

∎

15.3
Analysis of Cost of
\mathcal{W}

After termination of
Develop-Centers
, the algorithm lets
\mathcal{V}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}+(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)})
and calls
Rand-Local-Search
(\mathcal{V},|\mathcal{V}|-k)
to get
\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 15.10
.

We have,

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)\leq 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}).

Proof.

According to
Lemma

15.9
,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}+(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}-\mathcal{P}^{(0)}),\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)},\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k+l+1}(\mathcal{P}^{(0)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}).

∎

Lemma 15.11
.

We have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)\leq 32\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}).

Proof.

With guarantee of
Rand-Local-Search
in
Lemma

11.1
, and with
Lemma

15.10
, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 20\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})+12\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 32\beta\ \textnormal{{OPT}}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)})

∎

15.4
Analysis of Cost of
Robustify

Finally, we analyze the cost of
Robustify
on
\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 15.12

(Lemma 7.5 in
[
FLNS21
]
)
.

Assume
\mathcal{W}
is a set of
k
centers. If
\mathcal{U}
is the output of
Robustify
on
\mathcal{W}
, then

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}^{(l+1)}\right).

Proof.

By
Lemma

13.1
,
Robustify
calls
Make-Robust
on each center at most once.
Let
r
be the number of centers in
\mathcal{W}
for which a call to
Make-Robust
has happened.
Assume

\mathcal{W}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}
is an ordering by the time that
Make-Robust
is called on the centers
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{r}
and the last elements are ordered arbitrarily.
Assume also that
\mathcal{U}=\{w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{k}\}
where
w_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained by the call
Make-Robust
on
w_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
and
w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}
for each
r+1\leq i\leq k
.

For every
1\leq j\leq r
, integer
t(w_{j}^{\prime})
is the smallest integer satisfying

10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}\geq d(w_{j},\{w_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,w_{j-1}^{\prime},w_{j+1},\ldots,w_{k}\})/100.

Hence,

10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}\leq d(w_{j},w_{i}^{\prime})/10\quad\forall i<j

(74)

and

10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}\leq d(w_{j},w_{i})/10\quad\forall i>j.

(75)

Now, for every
1\leq i<j\leq r
, we conclude

10\cdot 10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}\leq d(w_{i}^{\prime},w_{j})\leq d(w_{i},w_{j})+d(w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime})\leq d(w_{i},w_{j})+10^{t(w_{i}^{\prime})}/2\leq\frac{11}{10}\ d(w_{i},w_{j}).

The first inequality holds by
Equation

74
, the third one holds by
Lemma

10.3
(note that
w_{i}^{\prime}=

Make-Robust
(w_{i})
) and the last one holds by
Equation

75
(by exchanging
i
and
j
since
i<j
).
This concludes for each
i<j
, we have
d(w_{i},w_{j})>2\cdot 10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}
.
The same inequality holds for each
i>j
by
Equation

75
.
As a result,

d(w_{i},w_{j})>2\cdot 10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}
for all
i\neq j
.
This concludes

\text{Ball}_{10^{t(w_{j}^{\prime})}}^{\mathcal{P}}(w_{j})\subseteq C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P}),

for every
1\leq j\leq r
.
By applying
Lemma

10.4
, we have

\textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j}^{\prime},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\ \textnormal{{Cost}}\left(w_{j},C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})\right)\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq r.

(76)

Finally, since
C_{w_{j}}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{P})
for
1\leq j\leq k
form a partition of
